Root Number Equidistribution for Self-Dual Automorphic Representations on GL N \mathrm{GL}_{N}
Rahul Dalal 1, Mathilde Gerbelli-Gauthier 2

Abstract
. Let F F be a totally real field. We study the root numbers ϵ ⁡ ( 1 / 2 , π ) \epsilon(1/2,\pi) of self-dual cuspidal automorphic representations π \pi of GL 2 ​ N / F \mathrm{GL}_{2N}/F with conductor 𝔫 \mathfrak{n} and regular integral infinitesimal character λ \lambda . If π \pi is orthogonal, then ϵ ⁡ ( 1 / 2 , π ) \epsilon(1/2,\pi) is known to be identically one. We show that for symplectic representations, the root numbers ϵ ⁡ ( 1 / 2 , π ) \epsilon(1/2,\pi) equidistribute between ± 1 \pm 1 as λ → ∞ \lambda\to\infty , provided that there exists a prime dividing 𝔫 \mathfrak{n} with power > N >N .We also study conjugate self-dual representations with respect to a CM extension E / F E/F , where we obtain a similar result under the assumption that 𝔫 \mathfrak{n} is divisible by a large enough power of a ramified prime and provide evidence that equidistribution does not hold otherwise. In cases where there are known to be associated Galois representations (e.g by [ Shi11 ] ), we deduce root number equidistribution results for the corresponding families of N N -dimensional Galois representations. The proof generalizes a classical argument for the case of GL 2 / ℚ \mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q} by using Arthur’s trace formula and the endoscopic classification for quasisplit classical groups similarly to a previous work ( [ DGG22 ] ). The main new technical difficulty is evaluating endoscopic transfers of the required test functions at central elements.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Context

Recall a classical open question: consider the set of elliptic curves
E/\mathbb{Q}
with conductor
\leq n
. The BSD conjecture leads us to expect that the parity of the rank of the group of
\mathbb{Q}
-points of
E
depends on the root number
\epsilon(1/2,E)=\pm 1
of the corresponding
L
-function. We can therefore ask whether this root number is equidistributed between
\pm 1
as
n\to\infty
. This paper studies an automorphic, weight-aspect variant of this question for higher-rank groups. Under very mild technical assumptions, we prove that equidistribution holds in all cases where it should be expected, see Theorem
1.2.1
.

1.1.1.
Automorphic Translation

By the modularity theorem, every elliptic curve
E/\mathbb{Q}
of conductor
n
corresponds to a cuspidal, weight-
2
modular (eigen)newform
f
of level
n
with matching
L
-function/root number:
\epsilon(1/2,E)=\epsilon(1/2,f)
. We can therefore ask instead if the root numbers of cuspidal weight-
2
modular newforms of level
n
equidistribute as
n\to\infty
. This does not amount to computing the same statistic for rational elliptic curves since there are many extra modular forms. However, it is more natural from some perspectives
: by the modularity theorem, cuspidal weight-
2
newforms are in bijection with irreducible
2
-dimensional representations of
\Gal(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
with Hodge-Tate weights
\{0,1\}
. These counts may be more relevant in certain contexts.

Most importantly for our purposes, counting modular forms opens the way for the application of trace formula techniques. These have been used to prove equidistribution in
[
SZ88
,
Mar18
,
Mar23
]
and are effective enough to compute finer statistics such as lower-order biases as in the results of Martin. Trace formulas even allow for computing subtle correlations between root numbers and Hecke eigenvalues depending on second-order estimates—for example, the “murmurations” phenomenon proven in
[
Zub23
]
.

1.1.2.
Generalization to Higher Dimensions

Recall that cuspidal modular newforms correspond to certain automorphic representations
\pi
on
\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q}
with regular, integral infinitesimal character at infinity. Any automorphic representation factors as a restricted direct product over places
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
:

\pi={\bigotimes_{v}}^{\prime}\pi_{v},

with each
\pi_{v}
an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
. In this context, the generalization of weight of a modular form is the so-called infinitesimal character of
\pi_{\infty}
.
The automorphic representations corresponding to holomorphic modular forms are those where
\pi_{\infty}
has regular, integral infinitesimal character—i.e. the same as a finite-dimensional irreducible representation and therefore as “simple” as possible. Furthermore, as pointed out in
[
BG14
]
, having integral infinitesimal character is exactly the expected condition needed to have an associated Galois representation. Thus automorphic representations of
GL_{N}/\mathbb{Q}
with regular, integral infinitesimal character appear to be a natural generalization of holomorphic modular forms.

Generalizations of modular forms actually satisfy one more condition. For automorphic representations
\pi
on
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
, having regular integral infinitesimal character at infinity is equivalent to

\pi_{\infty}

being either discrete series or trivial. This discrete-at-infinity requirement can be thought of as a
“niceness” condition; that these automorphic representations are the simplest possible in the same way that modular forms of weight
\geq 2
are simpler than Maass forms or weight-
1
forms. Unfortunately, for
N>2
,
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{R})
has no discrete series, so it at first appears that modular forms do not actually generalize to higher dimension.

A good resolution is to
consider automorphic representations
\pi
that are either:

•

self-dual

:
\pi
is an automorphic representation for
\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F
for
F
a totally real number field and
\pi^{\vee}\cong\pi
.

•

conjugate self-dual

:
\pi
is an automorphic representation for
\mathrm{GL}_{N}/E
for
E/F
a CM quadratic extension of number fields and
\pi^{\vee}\cong\bar{\pi}
(i.e. the
E/F
-conjugate).

Any (conjugate) self-dual
\pi
has component
\pi_{\infty}
that is also (conjugate) self-dual.
Moreover, such
\pi_{\infty}
with regular enough and integral infinitesimal character are discrete within the (conjugate) self-dual spectrum. Lastly, these are also the automorphic representations to which, even in higher dimension, there are expected to be attached Galois representations—e.g. by work of Shin and collaborators
[
Shi11
]
,
[
KS20
]
,
[
KS23
]
in some cases and
[
Shi24
]
for a “weak transfer” result in all cases.

Self-dual automorphic representations can be classified into two types: orthogonal- or symplectic-type. This should be thought of as encoding whether the
conjectural associated Galois representations preserve a symmetric or skew-symmetric bilinear form (the precise definition will be recalled in §
2.3
and depends on
[
Art13
]
). One of the outputs of the endoscopic classification
[
Art13
]
is that orthogonal-type automorphic representations have root number determined by their central character, and as such, easily understood. In the self-dual case, the most interesting statistics are those of the symplectic-type automorphic representations. We similarly restrict our attention to the conjugate-symplectic case for conjugate self-dual representations, this time justified by results in the unitary endoscopic classification
[
Mok15
]
.

Note that in the self-dual case, restriction to symplectic-type is also natural from the Galois side. The natural generalization of elliptic curves is higher-dimensional abelian varieties. Just like elliptic curves, these are conjecturally associated to automorphic representations through their Galois representations. However, Galois representations of abelian varieties always preserve the symplectic Weil pairing, so their associated automorphic representations should
be symplectic-type.

In summary, it is most appropriate to consider the following generalization of modular forms: (conjugate) symplectic-type automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
with regular, integral infinitesimal character at infinity.

1.2.
Results

1.2.1.
Setup

To make the results precise, fix a totally real number field
F
. We consider two cases:

•

self-dual: define
G_{N}=\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F
,

•

conjugate self-dual: define
G_{N}=\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}
.

Let
\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G_{N})
be the set of cuspidal automorphic representations for
G_{N}
—i.e those on
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F)\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
in the self-dual case and those on
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(E)\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{E})
in the conjugate self-dual case.

As discussed in §
1.1.2
and made precise in §
2.3
, there is a subset of
\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G_{N})
of (conjugate) self-dual representations which can be partitioned in to (conjugate) symplectic and (conjugate) orthogonal pieces. Call these
\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G_{N})
and
\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{orth}}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G_{N})
respectively.

Any
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G_{N})
has two associated invariants: its infinitesimal character
\lambda
depending on
\pi_{\infty}
that is a tuple of complex numbers
\lambda_{v}
for each
v|\infty
(see §
5.1
), and its conductor
\mathfrak{n}
depending on
\pi^{\infty}
that is an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
(see §
3.1
). For
\star=\text{orth, symp}
, let
\mathcal{AR}^{\star}_{N}(\lambda,\mathfrak{n})
be the subset of
\mathcal{AR}^{\star}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G_{N})
with the corresponding invariants. Following the discussion of §
1.1.2
, we only consider regular integral
\lambda
(see §
5.1.2
).

Finally, every (conjugate) self-dual representation
\pi
has a root number invariant
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)=\pm 1
(see §
3.3.2
for normalization details). We are interested in the average value

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\to\infty\\
\lambda\text{ integral}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{AR}^{\star}_{N}(\lambda,\mathfrak{n})|}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\star}_{N}(\lambda,\mathfrak{n})}\epsilon(1/2,\pi).

for
\star=\mathrm{orth},\mathrm{symp}
and where
\lambda\to\infty
is as a dominant weight of
G_{\infty}
, i.e. so that the minimum difference between elements in each tuple
\lambda_{v}
goes to
\infty
; see §
5.6
.

1.2.2.
Main Theorem

First, by the endoscopic classifications
[
Art13
]
and
[
Mok15
]
, the root number
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)
of
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{orth}}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G_{N})
depends only on the central character of
\pi
. Therefore, we focus on the symplectic-type representations. We also assume that
N
is even, as this restriction applies only in the conjugate self-dual case since there are no self-dual, symplectic-type representations with
N
odd.

Our main result is that
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)
equidistributes under these conditions:

Theorem 1.2.1

(
10.2.1
,
10.3.2
)
.

Let
N
be even and let
\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{v}\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{e_{v}}
be such that:

•

One of the
e_{v}
is either odd or greater than
N
(self-dual case),

•

One of the
e_{v}
is large enough for one of the ramified
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
and
\mathfrak{n}
is coprime to
2
(conjugate self-dual case).

Then

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\to\infty\\
\lambda\text{ integral}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(\lambda,\mathfrak{n})|}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(\lambda,\mathfrak{n})}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)\to 0.

Furthermore, in the self-dual case, the conditions on
\mathfrak{n}
are also necessary.

Theorems
10.2.1
and
10.3.2
are in fact stronger: they show that averages of
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)
are
0
even when weighted by
\tr_{\pi_{S}}(f_{S})
for any “positive” unramified test function
f_{S}
at a finite set of places
S
coprime to
\mathfrak{n}
: see Definition
10.1.2
. This is analogous to weighting the average on
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
by a classical Hecke eigenvalue. The main theorems also provide a bound on the rate of convergence in terms of this weighting.

Finally, by Conjecture
8.4.3
, we expect the precise conditions on
\mathfrak{n}
in Theorem
10.3.2
to also be necessary conditions as long as there is no obstruction to the existence of
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(\lambda,\mathfrak{n})
coming from the central character of
\pi^{\infty}
.

Note
.

It is somewhat surprising that equidistribution in this conjugate self-dual setting seems to only holds at ramified level. In fact, checking examples on
[
LMF
]
seems to suggest that the root number of a local conjugate self-dual representation over an unramified quadratic extension is determined by its conductor.

1.2.3.
Side Effects

Our techniques have two interesting side effects:

First, one of the key technical tools is a “weight-aspect” version of the bounds in
[
DGG22
]
that also applies to the self-dual setting (§
5
,
6
). This is of independent interest—for example, Theorem
6.5.1
is a key input in proving that the gate sets for quantum circuits constructed in
[
DEP
]
achieve the bounds needed to be so-called “golden gates”.

Second, Theorem
9.1.2
roughly states that the Plancherel measure on a
p
-adic quasisplit classical group
G_{v}
is “constant” on
L
-packets. In particular, this shows that all elements of a given supercuspidal
L
-packet for
G_{v}
have the same formal degree (Corollary
9.1.3
).

1.2.4.
Methods and Potential Extensions

We rely on generalizations of the classical trace formulas that were so effective in studying root numbers of modular forms
[
Mar23
,
Zub23
]
. Specifically, through the stabilization of the
twisted
trace formula as used in the endoscopic classifications of
[
Art13
]
and
[
Mok15
]
, we produce an expression for our desired statistic made up of terms as in
[
Art89
]
, which have further been studied and bounded in
[
ST16
]
. This analysis builds on the authors’ previous works
[
Dal22
]
and
[
DGG22
]
. It should be compared to an alternate, non-endoscopic strategy for analyzing the twisted trace formula in
[
TW24
]
.

These more intricate methods currently come with serious costs (beyond just the length of the argument). First, the very precise bounds needed for the finer estimates of
[
Mar23
]
and especially
[
Zub23
]
depend on interpretations of terms on the geometric side of the trace formula in terms of
L
-values/class numbers. These are currently only available in the case of
untwisted

\mathrm{GL}_{N}
from
[
Yun13
]
.

Second, it would be desirable to have asymptotics in the level aspect
\mathfrak{n}\to\infty
in addition to
\lambda\to\infty
. The complexity of the test functions
\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
constructed in §
4
make this hard: we would require explicit asymptotics in
\mathfrak{n}
of their orbital integrals instead of just the very coarse information computed in §§
7
-
9
. We are still hopeful that the
\mathfrak{n}\to\infty
limit is tractable with more work and leave it for a future paper, possibly using techniques from
[
KY12
]
and
[
TW24
]
.

Finally, our techniques fall short in the conjugate self-dual case for
N
odd. Conceptually, we are really studying
(N-1)
st powers of root numbers by taking advantage of the particular simplicity of the
N\times(N-1)
Rankin-Selberg integral representation: that the involution on functions that proves this functional equation is “geometric”—i.e. comes from an involution of the group instead of something like a Fourier transform in the case of Godement-Jacquet. Our parity restriction is also related to the miraculous vanishing of signs in twisted endoscopic transfers for odd
N
that was key to the main result of
[
AOY22
]
. In our context, these signs exactly carry the information of root numbers we want to probe, so their vanishing is a tragedy instead of a miracle.

1.3.
Application to Galois Representations

In the conjugate self-dual case when
N
is even and
\lambda
is integral,
[
Shi11
]
attaches to each
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(\mathfrak{n},\lambda)
a Galois representation of conductor
\mathfrak{n}
, Hodge-Tate weights matching
\lambda
, and the same root number. Let
\overline{\Phi}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)
be the resulting set of conjugate symplectic-type Galois representations
\Gamma_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}
.

We deduce from our main theorems an equidistribution result for the family
\overline{\Phi}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)
:

Corollary 1.3.1
.

Let
N
be even and assume that
\mathfrak{n}
is divisible by a sufficiently large power of a ramified prime. Then

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\to\infty\\
\lambda\text{ integral}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\overline{\Phi}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)|}\sum_{\varphi\in\overline{\Phi}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)}\epsilon(1/2,\varphi)\to 0.

Assuming the Langlands correspondence, we expect
\overline{\Phi}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)
to be exactly the set of irreducible Galois representations of specific Hodge-Tate weight and conductor. This gives:

Corollary 1.3.2
.

Let
N
be even and assume the Langlands correspondence for conjugate symplectic-type automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}/E
with regular integral infinitesimal character at infinity.

Then, if
\Phi_{N}^{\mathrm{symp}}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)
is the set of irreducible
N
-dimensional Galois representations that are

•

conjugate symplectic,

•

conductor
\mathfrak{n}
coprime to
2
divisible by a high enough power of a ramified prime,

•

Hodge-Tate weights matching
\lambda
,

we have,

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\to\infty\\
\lambda\text{ integral}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\Phi^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)|}\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(E/F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)}\epsilon(1/2,\varphi)\to 0

Known analogous self-dual results,
[
KS20
]
,
[
KS23
]
, do not yet cover the symplectic-type case—in particular, the weak matching of
[
Shi24
]
does not yet give matching of root numbers. Therefore we only get a Galois representation corollary conditional on the Langlands correspondence:

Corollary 1.3.3
.

Let
N
be even and assume the Langlands correspondence for symplectic-type automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F
with regular integral infinitesimal character at infinity.

Let
\Phi_{N}^{\mathrm{symp}}(F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)
be the set of irreducible symplectic Galois representations
\Gal(\bar{F}/F)\to\mathrm{Sp}_{N}
that have

•

conductor
\mathfrak{n}
divisible by a sufficiently large power of some prime,

•

Hodge-Tate weights matching
\lambda
,

Then

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\to\infty\\
\lambda\text{ integral}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\Phi^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)|}\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi^{\mathrm{symp}}_{N}(F,\mathfrak{n},\lambda)}\epsilon(1/2,\varphi)\to 0.

if and only if there is finite place
v
such that
v(\mathfrak{n})>N
or
v(\mathfrak{n})
is odd.

As in Theorem
1.2.1
, we can weight the averages by functions of Frobenius conjugacy classes at places coprime to
\mathfrak{n}
, and we maintain the same control over rates of convergence—see again Theorems
10.2.1
and
10.3.2
for full generality.

1.4.
Summary of Argument

Once the outline of the argument in §
1.4.2
is understood well, the details of the individual steps are reasonably self-contained and can more-or-less be read in any order.

1.4.1.
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-model

Our method is very closely modeled on the classical
\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q}
-argument which we now present in modernized language to emphasize the parallelism.

Let
\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)
be the set of automorphic representations for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q}
with regular, integral infinitesimal character
\lambda
at infinity. Our goal is to study the asymptotic fractions of representations
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)
with conductor
c(\pi)=n
and
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)=\pm 1
—in other words, the ratio

(1.4.1)

\left.\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)\\
c(\pi)=n\end{subarray}}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)\right/\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)\\
c(\pi)=n\end{subarray}}1.

as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Step 1:

Following the theory of newvectors, there exists a sequence of compact-open subgroups
\Gamma_{0}(n)\subset GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})
such that

(\pi^{\infty})^{\Gamma_{0}(n)}=\begin{cases}1&c(\pi)=n\\
0&c(\pi)>n.\end{cases}

If
c(\pi)=n
, we call an appropriately normalized element of
(\pi^{\infty})^{\Gamma_{0}(n)}
the newvector
\varphi
for
\pi
. The newvector satisfies a key property of being a “test vector” for Rankin-Selberg integral representations.

Step 2:

The conductor
n
has an associated Atkin-Lehner involution
\iota_{n}
. As a corollary of the proof of the functional equation of the
L
-function
L(s,\pi)
through its
\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1}
-Rankin-Selberg integral representation (e.g.
[
DS05
, 5.10.2]
), we get

\varphi^{\iota_{n}}=\epsilon^{\infty}(1/2,\pi)\varphi

(leaving out an archimedean factor). The “test vector” property of the newvector
\varphi
is a crucial input here.

Step 3:

We can find
g_{n}\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})
such that
f^{\iota_{n}}=g_{n}f
for all
f
in the space of automorphic forms. By taking the trace of automorphic representations
\pi
against the volume-normalized indicator functions of the corresponding cosets, we get:

\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{\Gamma_{0}(n)}=\begin{cases}0&c(\pi)>n\\
1&c(\pi)=n\end{cases},\qquad\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{g_{n}\Gamma_{0}(n)}=\begin{cases}0&c(\pi)>n\\
\epsilon^{\infty}(1/2,\pi)&c(\pi)=n\end{cases},

where the contributions from the archimedean place are omitted.

Step 4:

Using an explicit description of the space of oldforms—the
\Gamma_{0}(n^{\prime})
-fixed points in
\pi^{\infty}
when
c(\pi)=n
and
n|n^{\prime}
—we compute

\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{\Gamma_{0}(n^{\prime})}=\dim((\pi^{\infty})^{\Gamma_{0}(n^{\prime})}),\qquad\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{g_{n}\Gamma_{0}(n^{\prime})}.

The values depend only on
n^{\prime}/n
. This allows us to use linear combination of functions of the type
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{\Gamma_{0}(n)}
or
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{g_{n}\Gamma_{0}(n)}
to construct functions
E^{\infty}_{n}
and
C^{\infty}_{n}
respectively so that

\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}C^{\infty}_{n}=\begin{cases}1&c(\pi)=n\\
0&c(\pi)\neq n\\
\end{cases},\qquad\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}E^{\infty}_{n}=\begin{cases}\epsilon^{\infty}(1/2,\pi)&c(\pi)=n\\
0&c(\pi)\neq n\\
\end{cases}.

Since
\epsilon_{\infty}(\pi_{\infty},1/2)=:\epsilon_{\infty}(1/2,\lambda)
is constant for
\pi_{\infty}
with fixed infinitesimal character, we can express the quantity in (
1.4.1
), which we aim to estimate, as the following ratio of traces:

\epsilon_{\infty}(1/2,\lambda)\left.\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(E^{\infty}_{n})\right/\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(C^{\infty}_{n}).

Step 5:

The Eichler-Selberg trace formula gives a tractable expression for

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(\psi)

for any test function
\psi
. Using this expression, we show that if
F_{\lambda}
is the finite-dimensional representation of
GL_{2}
with infinitesimal character
\lambda
, then

(\dim F_{\lambda})^{-1}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q},\lambda)}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(\psi)\to\psi(1)

as
\lambda\to\infty
. In particular the expression (
1.4.1
) has asymptotics:

\eqref{eq gl2goal}\to\epsilon_{\infty}(1/2,\lambda)\frac{E^{\infty}_{n}(1)}{C^{\infty}_{n}(1)}.

Step 6:

We compute that
C_{n}(1)>0
. Furthermore,
E_{n}(1)=0
if and only if
n
is not a product of squares of distinct primes. This shows that we have asymptotic equidistribution of
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)
if and only if
n
is not product of squares of distinct primes.

1.4.2.
Full Argument

We now summarize the general
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
-argument and point out where complications occur. We are interested in the analogous

(1.4.2)

\left.\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F,\lambda)\\
c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}\end{subarray}}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)\right/\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F,\lambda)\\
c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}\end{subarray}}1.

for totally real
F
in the self-dual case and

(1.4.3)

\left.\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N},\lambda)\\
c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}\end{subarray}}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)\right/\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}^{\mathrm{symp}}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N},\lambda)\\
c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}\end{subarray}}1.

for
E/F
CM in the conjugate self-dual case. Note here that over a general number field, the level becomes an ideal
\mathfrak{n}
of
F
instead of an integer
n
.

Step 1:

Newvector theory directly generalizes to
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
or
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{E})
through
[
JPSS81
]
. The corresponding level subgroups are the “mirahorics”
K_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
. We summarize the results and the necessary “test vector” properties in §§
3.1
,
3.2
.

Step 2:

As in
[
Tom24
, §4.2]
, we generalize the Atkin-Lehner involution to define involutions
\iota_{\mathfrak{n}},\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}
in the self-dual or conjugate self-dual cases respectively. To connect this to root numbers, it turns out to be best to look at
\mathrm{GL}_{N}\times\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}
Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions instead of those associated to
\mathrm{GL}_{N}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1}
. The proof of the functional equation (e.g.
[
Cog04
, §6]
) can be algebraically manipulated to involve either
\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}
or
\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}
instead of the standard involution
g\mapsto g^{-T}
. This roughly shows that for newvectors
\varphi
in (conjugate) self-dual representations,

(1.4.4)

\varphi^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}\approx\epsilon^{\infty}(1/2,\pi)^{N-1}\varphi.

After reviewing some background in §
3.3
, this is work of §§
3.4
,
3.5
culminating in local Theorems
3.4.2
and
3.5.2
.

The exponent in (
1.4.4
) is the reason for our restriction to even
N
in the conjugate self-dual result. Luckily, there are no odd symplectic-type representations in the self-dual case.

Step 3:

This is where we make our main departure from the classical argument. Since
\iota_{\mathfrak{n}},\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}
are inner to
g\mapsto g^{-T}
and
g\mapsto\bar{g}^{-T}
which is outer when
N>2
, the element
g_{\mathfrak{n}}
such that

g_{\mathfrak{n}}\varphi=\varphi^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}

no longer lies
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
itself, but instead in a larger twisted group. Consider involutions
\theta,\bar{\theta}
that are inner to
g\mapsto g^{-T}
,
g\mapsto\bar{g}^{-T}
respectively. Then
g_{\mathfrak{n}}
lies in one of the cosets
\widetilde{G}_{N}:=\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{F})\rtimes\theta
or
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{E})\rtimes\bar{\theta}
in a corresponding semidirect product to which the action of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
on
\pi
can be extended. See §
4.1
for details.

Therefore, the generalized versions of the building blocks
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{g_{n}K_{1}(n)}
for
E_{n}
lie on the twisted group
\widetilde{G}_{N}
instead. This is a feature and not a bug since it allows us to use the twisted trace formula and isolate the (conjugate) self-dual spectrum for
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
as desired.

On the other hand, generalizing the building blocks
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K_{1}(n)}
of
C_{n}
is much more difficult since we do not have a twisted analogue. We will therefore need to construct a generalized
C_{n}
in another way.

Step 4:

A result of Reeder
[
Ree91
]
gives a description of oldform spaces
(\pi^{\infty})^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})}
for
c(\pi)|\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
in terms of Hecke operators for
\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}
. The action of these
\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}
-Hecke operators can be commuted with the
\mathfrak{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}
’s to give a formula for
\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(\mathbf{1}_{g_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}K_{1}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})})
that only depends on
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}/c(\pi)
(from local Propositions
4.2.3
,
4.2.5
).

In particular, we can similarly solve a system of linear equations and find a linear combination
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
of the twisted test functions
\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{g_{\mathfrak{n}}K_{1}(\mathfrak{n})}
on
\widetilde{G}_{N}
so that roughly:

\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})\approx\begin{cases}\epsilon^{\infty}(1/2,\pi)&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}\\
0&c(\pi)\neq\mathfrak{n}\end{cases}

This is the work of §§
4.2
,
4.3
culminating in Theorems
4.3.5
and
4.3.6
.

The twisted generalization of
C^{\infty}_{n}
requires a completely different method in §
4.4
. We first show that root numbers are constant on Bernstein components of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
intersect the set of (conjugate) self-dual representations of a fixed conductor (Propositions
4.4.2
,
4.4.3
). Therefore, an abstract Paley-Wiener theorem
[
Rog81
]
lets us bootstrap the existence of
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
into the existence of a
\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
such that

\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=\begin{cases}1&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}\\
0&c(\pi)\neq\mathfrak{n}\end{cases}

(Corollary
4.4.4
).

Step 5:

The techniques of
[
DGG22
]
using the inductive argument of
[
Taï17
]
modify in a straightforward way to generate a tractable expression for both

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{symp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F,\lambda)}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(\widetilde{\psi})

and

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{symp}}(\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N},\lambda)}\tr_{\pi^{\infty}}(\widetilde{\psi}),

for suitable twisted test functions
\widetilde{\psi}
(Proposition
6.1.1
and §
5
).

With the bounds from
[
ST16
]
, we find that, up to technical issues with centers, both expressions have scaling limits:

\approx\widetilde{\psi}^{G}(1),

for
\widetilde{\psi}^{G}
an endoscopic transfer to the twisted endoscopic group
G=\mathrm{SO}_{N+1}
of
\widetilde{G}_{N}
in the self-dual case and
G=U_{N}
of
\widetilde{G}_{N}
embedded through base change in the conjugate self-dual case (§
6
culminating in Theorem
6.4.1
).

In total, we get that, up to center technicalities:

\eqref{eq sdgoal},\eqref{eq csdgoal}\overset{\approx}{\to}\epsilon_{\infty}(1/2,\lambda)\frac{(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}(1)}{(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}(1)}

as
\lambda\to\infty
. Note that
[
ST16
]
also gives us a power saving on the error term.

Step 6:

It remains to understand
(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}(1)
and
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}(1)
. Unlike the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
case, this is by far the hardest part of the argument.

Our explicit description for
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
allows us to use the orbital integral transfer identities to compute
(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}(1)
. The identity class of our relevant
G
is a twisted analogue of the “equisingular” classes studied in
[
LS07
, 2.4.A]
. Therefore in §
7.1
, we recall a Shalika germ argument from Mœglin and Waldspurger’s stabilization of the twisted trace formula
[
MW16
]
to get that
(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}(1)
equals a particular non-regular twisted
\kappa
-orbital integral on
\widetilde{G}_{N}
(Corollary
7.1.8
). Computing when this integral vanishes is the work of §
7.2
,
8
and is somewhat involved in the conjugate self-dual case.

The function
\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
is much more daunting since it only exists by an abstract Paley-Wiener theorem. Luckily, we only need to show that
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}(1)>0
(up to details with the center). By the endoscopic character identities and the spectral definition of
\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
, we can show that a formally defined “stable Fourier transform” of
(\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}
is always positive and somewhere non-vanishing. This allows us to apply a positivity criterion (Proposition
9.2.2
) that we prove from a “stable Fourier inversion” result (Theorem
9.1.2
).

We obtain this stable Fourier inversion result by showing that the Plancherel measure for each
G_{v}
descends to a formally-defined “stable unitary dual” in a key Proposition
9.1.1
. We first interpret the Plancherel measure of a local test function
\widehat{\psi}_{v}
on the unitary dual as an asymptotic count of global automorphic representations. By ideas from
[
DGG22
]
, this is then shown to match a stable trace formula asymptotic which necessarily only depends on the stable Fourier transform of
\psi_{v}
. This is the work of §
9.1
. We conclude the positivity statement in §
9.2
.

That the stable Fourier transform is somewhere non-vanishing requires number-theoretic input: the existence of local (conjugate) self-dual representations with desired invariants—type, conductor, central character, etc. This is done in §
9.3
and is again quite involved in the conjugate self-dual case. Our final statements for
\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
are in §
9.4
.

1.5.
Conditionality

Since we need to separate (conjugate) symplectic from (conjugate) orthogonal global representations with the trace formula, this result is conditional on the endoscopic classification—
[
Art13
]
for the self-dual case and
[
Mok15
]
for the conjugate self-dual. These classifications depend on the unpublished weighted, twisted fundamental lemma.

We note that the dependence of
[
Art13
]
on its unpublished references A25-27 and
[
Mok15
]
on their unitary analogues has recently been resolved by
[
AGI+24
]
.

1.6.
Acknowledgments

The idea for this paper came from watching talks on the IAS YouTube channel about murmurations of families of elliptic curves and how they could be studied with the trace formula—we would like to thank the IAS for making them publicly available. Yiannis Sakellaridis pointed out how higher-rank Rankin-Selberg integrals would be a good way to generalize the classical
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
argument relating root numbers to Atkin-Lehner involutions, and Radu Toma alterted us to a important sign mistake in an earlier version. We would also like to thank Alexander Bertoloni-Meli, Peter Dillery, Peter Humphries, Ashwin Iyengar, Arno Kret, Huajie Li, Kimball Martin, Masao Oi, David Schwein, Sug Woo Shin, Joel Specter, and Sandeep Varma for helpful conversations.

The first author was supported by NSF postdoctoral grant 2103149 while working on this project.

Groups Considered

Throughout this paper we will be looking at two cases of involution/self-duality on
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
. Summarizing our parallel notational conventions for each case:

The self-dual or orthogonal/symplectic case where we consider:

2.
Self-Dual and Conjugate Self-Dual Representations

We will consider, in parallel, self-dual and conjugate self-dual automorphic representations of
GL_{N}
, and their local constituents.

2.1.
Local Notions

Let
F
be a local field and
\pi
be a unitary irreducible representation of
GL_{N}(F)
. The dual
\pi^{\vee}
of
\pi
is the representation acting on the same space, but such that

\pi^{\vee}(g)=\pi(g^{-T}).

2.1.1.
Self-dual representations

We will say that
\pi
is self-dual if
\pi\simeq\pi^{\vee}
. In this case,
\pi
corresponds under the local Langlands correspondence
[
HT99
,
Hen00
,
Sch13
]
to an
N
-dimensional representation
\rho
of the Local Langlands group
L_{F}
, i.e. a representation such that

\rho(\sigma)=\rho(\sigma)^{-T}\quad\sigma\in L_{F}.

If
\rho
is additionally irreducible, then it preserves a nondegenerate bilinear form
B
. We say that
\rho
is
orthogonal
or
symplectic
depending on whether
B
can be chosen to be. We correspondingly say that
\pi
is orthogonal-type or symplectic-type. If
\pi
is symplectic-type, then
N
is necessarily even, and the central character of
\pi
is trivial. When
\pi
is orthogonal-type, its central character is a quadratic character
\omega_{\pi}:F^{\times}\to\pm 1
. Note that being orthogonal and symplectic aren’t mutually exclusive.

If
\rho
is reducible but semisimple, we say it is orthogonal or symplectic if all its irreducible constituents are.

2.1.2.
Conjugate self-dual representations

Let
E/F
be a (possibly split) extension of local fields, with involution
x\mapsto\bar{x}
. We say that a representation
\pi
of
GL_{N}(E)
is
conjugate self-dual
if
\pi\simeq\bar{\pi}^{\vee}
where

\bar{\pi}^{\vee}(g)=\pi(\bar{g}^{-T}).

Such a representation
\pi
corresponds under the local Langlands correspondence to an
N
-dimensional conjugate self-dual representation
\rho
of
\Gamma_{E}
. That is, we have
\rho^{-T}\simeq\rho^{c}
where
\rho^{c}(\sigma):=\rho(w_{c}^{-1}\sigma w_{c})
for
w_{c}\in L_{E}\setminus L_{F}
.

If
\rho
is further irreducible, there is a bilinear form
B
on
\rho
such that for all
\sigma\in\Gamma_{E}

B(\rho^{c}(\sigma)x,\rho(\sigma)x)=B(x,y),\qquad B(x,y)=\eta B(y,\rho(w_{c}^{2})x)

for
\eta\pm 1
. If we can choose
\eta=1
, then
\rho
is
conjugate-orthogonal
and if we can choose
\eta=-1
,
\rho
is
conjugate-symplectic
.
Note that these conditions are not mutually exclusive. We accordingly say that
\pi
is orthogonal-type or symplectic-type.

If
\rho
is reducible but semisimple, we say it is conjugate orthogonal or conjugate symplectic if all its irreducible constituents are.

2.2.
Twisted Groups and Endoscopy

2.2.1.
Twisted Groups

Self-dual and conjugate self-dual representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
are best studied using twisted endoscopy. We first define the key twisted groups.

Over field
F
or quadratic extension
E/F
, let

G_{N}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{GL}_{N}&\text{self-dual case}\\
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}&\text{conjugate self-dual case.}\end{cases}

In the self-dual case, consider the involution

\iota:G_{N}:=\mathrm{GL}_{N}\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}:g\mapsto g^{-T}

over the number field
F
. In the conjugate self-dual case, consider

\bar{\iota}:G_{N}:=\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}\to\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}:g\mapsto\bar{g}^{-T},

also defined over
F
.

Next, define the matrix

(2.2.1)

w_{N}:=\begin{pmatrix}&&&(-1)^{N-1}\\
&&\iddots&\\
&-1&&\\
1&&&\end{pmatrix}

so that conjugating
\iota,\bar{\iota}
by
w_{N}
gives involutions
\theta,\bar{\theta}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
and
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}
respectively that preserve the standard pinnings.

Note
.

As computational reminders,
w_{N}^{-1}=(-1)^{N-1}w_{N}
and the automorphism
A\mapsto w_{N}A^{T}w_{N}^{-1}
reflects the entries of an
N\times N
matrix
A
along the anti-diagonal and then multiplies the
(i,j)
th entry by the “checkerboard pattern”
(-1)^{i+j}
.

Following
[
Art13
]
and
[
Mok15
]
, define twisted groups

\widetilde{G}_{N}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{GL}_{N}\rtimes\theta&\text{self-dual case}\\
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta}&\text{conjugate self-dual case.}\end{cases}

The (conjugate) self-dual representations of
G_{N}(\mathbb{A})
or
G_{N}(F_{v})
are exactly the ones that extend to
\widetilde{G}_{N}
. Any such irreducible representation has exactly two extensions, though in our cases we will choose one as canonical in section
4.1
.

2.2.2.
Twisted Endoscopic Groups

(Conjugate) self-dual automorphic representations can be studied through the twisted version of Arthur’s trace formula on
\widetilde{G}_{N}
as in
[
Art13
, §3]
or
[
Mok15
, §4]
. The trace formula decomposes into terms for elliptic endoscopic groups of
G_{N}
. We will only need to worry about the simple endoscopic groups
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
:

In the self-dual case, the simple twisted endoscopic groups
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
are (
[
Art13
, §1.2]
):

•

N
even:
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{N}
embedded in one possible way with trivial Galois action. This corresponds to
G\cong\mathrm{SO}_{N+1}
.

•

N
even:
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{SO}_{N}
with Galois action by
\Out(G)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
through the quadratic character
\eta
. There is a unique embedding for each
\eta
.
This corresponds to
G\cong\mathrm{SO}^{\eta}_{N}
, a quasisplit orthogonal group that is not split if
\eta\neq 1
.

•

N
odd:
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{SO}_{N}
with trivial Galois action. There is one possible embedding for each quadratic character
\eta
of
\Gamma_{F}
(equivalently, of
F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}
). This corresponds to
G\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{N-1}
. We denote the versions for different embeddings
\mathrm{Sp}^{\eta}_{N-1}
.

In the conjugate self dual case, they can be (
[
Mok15
, §2.4.1]
):

•

\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{GL}_{N}
with
\Gal(E/F)
acting through an automorphism inner to
g\mapsto g^{-T}
. This corresponds to
G\cong U^{E/F}_{N}
, the quasisplit unitary group. There are two possible embeddings
{}^{L}\!{G}\to{}^{L}\!{G}_{N}
, and we denote the endoscopic data associated to different embeddings by
U^{\pm}_{E/F}(N)
.

2.3.
Global Notions

2.3.1.
Self-Dual Case

Consider a number field
F
. A representation
\pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is self-dual if
\pi\cong\pi^{\vee}
. The central character
\omega_{\pi}
of
\pi
is then necessarily quadratic.

As one of the key outputs of the endoscopic classification
[
Art13
]
, every self-dual cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F
is assigned a group
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
. If the dual group
\widehat{G}
is symplectic, we call
\pi
symplectic-type and if
\widehat{G}
is orthogonal, we call
\pi
orthogonal-type.
As part of the result, whenever
\pi
is orthogonal/symplectic, then all its local factors
\pi_{v}
are too since they have parameters factoring through
G
.

Finally,
[
Art13
]
gives a criterion for distinguishing orthogonal vs. symplectic which can be taken as a more direct definition (though we will not use it):

Theorem 2.3.1

(
[
Art13
, Thm 1.5.3(a)]
)
.

Let
\pi
be a self-dual automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F
. If
\omega_{\pi}
is non-trivial, then the assigned
G
is
\mathrm{SO}_{N}^{\omega_{\pi}}
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{N-1}^{\omega_{\pi}}
(through class field theory) and
\pi
is therefore orthogonal.

Otherwise,
\pi
is symplectic if an only if its alternating square
L
-function
L(s,\pi,\wedge^{2})
has a pole at
s=1
and orthogonal if and only if its symmetric square
L
-function
L(s,\pi,\Sym^{2})
has a pole at
s=1
.

2.3.2.
Conjugate Self-Dual Case

Consider a quadratic extension
E/F
. A representation
\pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{E})
is conjugate self-dual if
\pi\circ\sigma\cong\pi^{\vee}
where
\sigma
is the non-trivial element of
\Gal(E/F)
.

As one of the key outputs of the endoscopic classification
[
Mok15
]
, every conjugate self-dual cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}
is assigned a group
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
. If this group is
U_{N}^{(-1)^{N-1}}
, we call
\pi
conjugate orthogonal. Otherwise, we call
\pi
conjugate symplectic. As part of the result, whenever
\pi
is conjugate orthogonal/symplectic, then all its local factors
\pi_{v}
are too since they have parameters factoring through
G
.

Similar to the above,
[
Mok15
]
gives a criterion for distinguishing conjugate orthogonal vs. conjugate symplectic which can be taken as a more direct definition (which we again will not use):

Theorem 2.3.2

(
[
Mok15
, Thm 2.5.4(a)]
)
.

Let
\pi
be conjugate self-dual automorphic representation of
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}
. Then
\pi
is conjugate symplectic if an only if its Asai
L
-function
L(s,\pi,\mathrm{Asai}^{-})
has a pole at
s=1
and conjugate orthogonal if and only if its Asai
L
-function
L(s,\pi,\mathrm{Asai}^{+})
has a pole at
s=1
.

2.3.3.
Restrictions on
F

Finally, to keep the “discreteness” at infinity analogy with
\mathrm{GL}_{2}/\mathbb{Q}
-modular forms, we will only study cases when the relevant
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
have discrete series at infinity. Therefore, we make the following assumption:

Assumption 2.3.3
.

In the conjugate self-dual case assume that
F
is totally real. In the conjugate self-dual case assume that
E/F
is CM: a totally imaginary extension of a totally real field.

Outside of the
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n}^{\eta}
case, Assumption
2.3.3
implies that
G
has discrete series at infinity. In the
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n}^{\eta}
case, Assumption
2.3.3
gives that
G_{\infty}
has discrete series if and only if for each
v|\infty
, the following holds:
\eta|_{\Gal_{F_{v}}}
is trivial if and only if
n
is even. As we will see in Proposition
5.3.1
, only these
G
will be relevant.

3.
New Vectors and Root Numbers

In this section, we exploit integral representations of
L
-functions to realize the root number of a symplectic automorphic
L
-function as the eigenvalue of a certain operator acting on the space of newforms. The main statements
3.4.4
,
3.5.4
are local, and in the next section we will consider global applications.

3.1.
New Vectors

3.1.1.
Basic Version

We recall the theory of newvectors for local representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
. A useful reference is
[
Hum20
, §3]
, though results there are stated more generally than we need here.

Let
v
be a place of our number field
F
and
K_{v}=\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

For each
i
, the mirahoric subgroup
K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{i})=K_{1,v}(i)\subseteq K_{v}
is

K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{i}):=K_{1,v}(i):=\left\{A\in K_{v}:A\cong\begin{pmatrix}*&\cdots&*&*\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
*&\cdots&*&*\\
0&\cdots&0&1\end{pmatrix}\pmod{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{i}}\right\}.

Given an
ideal
\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{v}\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{e_{v}}
of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
, then

K_{1}(\mathfrak{n}):=\prod_{v}K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{e_{v}})\subseteq\prod_{v}\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{v}).

Theorem 3.1.2

(
[
JPSS81
, Thm. 5]
)
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be a generic irreducible representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})
. Then there is
i_{\pi_{v}}\geq 0
such that

\dim\left((\pi_{v})^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}^{i})}\right)=\begin{cases}0&i<i_{\pi_{v}}\\
1&i=i_{\pi_{v}}\end{cases}.

Definition 3.1.3
.

We call
c(\pi_{v})=\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{i_{\pi_{v}}}

the conductor of
\pi_{v}
. The conductor
\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
of a global automorphic representation
\pi=\bigotimes_{v}^{\prime}\pi_{v}
is defined as

c(\pi):=\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{v}c(\pi_{v}).

As local shorthand, we will often say
c(\pi_{v})=i_{\pi_{v}}
.

Definition 3.1.4
.

If
\pi
is a global automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
, its newvector, defined only up to constant factor, is an element of the one-dimensional space
\pi^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pi})}
.

3.1.2.
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}
version

In the unitary case,
G_{N,v}
is one of the following:

•

If
v
is split,
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)\cong\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})^{2}
.

•

If
v
is inert or ramified,
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)\cong\mathrm{GL}_{N}(E_{w})
for
w
lying over
v
.

Applying Theorem
3.1.2
for
GL_{N}(F_{v})^{2}
and
GL_{N}(E_{w})
respectively lets us define local conductors as formal powers of
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
as follows:

•

If
v
is split: a pair
\mathfrak{p}_{w}^{i},\mathfrak{p}_{w^{\prime}}^{j}
for integral
i,j
and
w
,
w^{\prime}
above
v
. We represent this as
\mathfrak{p}_{w}^{(i,j)}
for ordered pair of integers
(i,j)
. If
\pi_{v}
is conjugate self-dual, then
i=j
so we can formally represent this as
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{i}
for a single integer
i
. We use shorthand
c(\pi_{v})=i
.

•

If
v
is inert: a single
\mathfrak{p}_{w}^{i}
which we represent formally by
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{i}
for integral
i
. We use shorthand
c(\pi_{v})=i

•

If
v
is ramified: a single
\mathfrak{p}_{w}^{2i}
which we represent formally as
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{i}
for half-integral
i
. We use shorthand
c(\pi_{v})=2i
.

These correspond to analogous subgroups
K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{w}^{i})=K_{1,v}(i)\subseteq G_{N,v}
that satisfy the dimension-one property of Theorem
3.1.2
. We similarly define newvectors.

The global conductor
c(\pi)
can therefore be thought of as an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
. If
\pi
is further conjugate self-dual, then
c(\pi)
can be represented as a formal product

c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{v}\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}

where
v
ranges over finite places of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
and the
k_{v}
are positive integers except when
v
is ramified in which case they can also be half-integers.

Note
.

Beware that there is a difference of a factor of
2
between our local and global indexing at ramified primes. These can be distinguished by local indexing always being by an integer and global indexing always being by an ideal.

3.2.
Rankin-Selberg Integral Representations

Our construction of test functions that pick out root numbers relies on the Rankin-Selberg integral for
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
. We recall this now as presented in
[
Cog04
]
, see also
[
Hum20
, §§2,3]
.

3.2.1.
Whittaker Functions

Consider the upper-triangular unipotent subgroup
U\subset GL_{N}
. Pick a nontrivial character
\psi
of
F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{\times}
and extend it to a character of
U(k)\backslash U(\mathbb{A})
through

\psi\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&\cdots&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1&x_{n-1}\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}=\psi(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}).

Then, for any automorphic function
\varphi
on
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
, we get a Whittaker function:

W_{\varphi}(g,\psi):=W_{\varphi}(g):=\int_{U(F)\backslash U(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(xg)\psi^{-1}(x)\,dx

in the Gelfand-Graev space
W(\psi)
of functions satisfying
W_{\varphi}(xg)=\psi(x)W_{\varphi}(g)
for all
x\in U(\mathbb{A})
. Through this transformation property, we think of
\varphi\mapsto W_{\varphi}
as attempting to embed
\pi
as a
G(\mathbb{A})
-rep into a “twisted quotient”
(U(\mathbb{A}),\psi)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
. If this map
\pi\to W(\psi)
is non-zero,
\pi
is called
generic
. It is well-known that all cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
are generic, and that the embedding
\pi\hookrightarrow W(\psi)
is unique if it exists.

Therefore, If
\varphi=\prod_{v}\varphi_{v}
is a pure tensor in
\pi=\bigotimes_{v}^{\prime}\pi_{v}
, we have a factorization

W_{\varphi}=:\prod_{v}W_{\varphi_{v}}

through a corresponding factorization of the unique
\pi\hookrightarrow W(\psi)
.

We similarly define
\widehat{W}_{\varphi}
by integration against
\psi
to get an element of
W(\psi^{-1})
.

3.2.2.
Integral Representations

Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F
and
GL_{N-1}/F
respectively. For any
\varphi\in\pi
and
\varphi^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}
, define the Rankin-Selberg integral:

(3.2.1)

I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}):=\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}(F)\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}(\mathbb{A})}\varphi\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\varphi^{\prime}(\mathbf{a})|\det\mathbf{a}|^{s-1/2}\,d\mathbf{a}.\\

Rankin-Selberg theory provides a factorization of (
3.2.1
): if both
\varphi
,
\varphi^{\prime}
are pure tensors, then

(3.2.2)

I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})=\prod_{v}I_{v}(s,W_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})\\
:=\prod_{v}\int_{U_{N-1,v}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}W_{\varphi_{v}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{v}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\widehat{W}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}(\mathbf{a}_{v})|\det\mathbf{a}_{v}|_{v}^{s-1/2}\,d\mathbf{a}_{v}.

Then as a special case of a theorem of Jacquet–Piatetskii-Shapiro–Shalika corrected by Matringe:

Theorem 3.2.1

(
[
JPSS81
, Thm (4)]
, corrected in
[
Mat13
, Cor 3.3]
)
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be a generic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
and
\varphi_{v}
its newvector. Let
\pi^{\prime}_{v}
be an unramified representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{v}
its unramified vector. Normalize both so that
W_{\varphi_{v}}(1)=W_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}(1)=1
. Then
I(s,W_{\varphi_{v}},W_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})=L(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})
.

Moreover, we have a global functional equation

(3.2.3)

I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})=I(1-s,\varphi^{\iota},(\varphi^{\prime})^{\iota}),

where
\varphi^{\iota}(g):=\varphi(g^{-T})=\varphi(w_{N}g^{-T})
, since
\varphi
is automorphic.

We also have a local functional equation (
[
Cog04
, Thm 6.2]
specialized to
N\times N-1
): define
W_{\varphi}^{\iota}(g):=W_{\varphi}(w_{N}g^{-T})
. Then,

(3.2.4)

I_{v}(1-s,W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})=\omega_{\pi^{\prime}_{v}}(-1)^{N-1}\gamma(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)I_{v}(s,W_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})

defining a
\gamma
-factor
\gamma(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)
that is necessarily a rational function in
q_{v}^{-s}
and doesn’t depend on
\varphi_{v},\varphi_{v}^{\prime}
.

3.3.
Root Numbers

3.3.1.
\epsilon
-factors and definition

The local functional equation (
3.2.4
) lets us define
\epsilon
-factors from the local
L
-functions:

(3.3.1)

\epsilon(s,\pi\times\pi^{\prime},\psi):=\frac{\gamma(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)L(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v})}{L(1-s,\pi_{v}^{\vee}\times(\pi^{\prime}_{v})^{\vee})}.

These are the factors whose product defines the global functional equation for
L
-functions:

L(s,\pi\times\pi^{\prime})=\epsilon(s,\pi\times\pi^{\prime})L(1-s,\pi^{\vee}\times(\pi^{\prime})^{\vee}).

Here

\epsilon(s,\pi\times\pi^{\prime})=\prod_{v}\epsilon(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)

is therefore necessarily independent of
\psi
.

Factors
\epsilon(s,\pi):=\epsilon(s,\pi,\mathrm{triv})
for a single representation
\pi
can be defined through a similar
\mathrm{GL}_{N}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1}
-theory. Then:

Theorem 3.3.1

(
[
Cog04
, Prop 6.5, Thm 6.3]
)
.

Let
\pi_{v}
have conductor
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}
. Then

\epsilon(s,\pi_{v},\psi)=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)q_{v}^{-k(s-1/2)}.

Globally,

(3.3.2)

\epsilon(s,\pi)=\epsilon(1/2,\pi)|c(\pi)|^{1/2-s},

which motivates:

Definition 3.3.2
.

If
\pi
is an automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
, let the root number of
\pi
be
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)
.

As a final useful formula, if
\omega_{\pi_{v}}
is the central character of
\pi_{v}
,

\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v}^{\vee},\psi)=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(-1).

3.3.2.
The (conjugate) self-dual case

We recall results about root numbers of (conjugate) self-dual representations (to translate statements from
[
GGP11
]
to the automorphic side, note that that
the determinant of the parameter of a representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
is the parameter of its central character).

Proposition 3.3.3

(
[
GGP11
, Thm 5.1(1)]
)
.

Let the representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})
be self-dual with trivial central character. Then
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)=\pm 1
and is independent of
\psi
.

Proposition 3.3.4

(
[
GGP11
, Thm 5.1(2)]
)
.

Let the representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
be conjugate self-dual. Then, if
\psi\circ\sigma=\psi^{-1}
,
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)=\pm 1
. This value is independent of
\psi
if either
N
is even or
\pi_{v}
is conjugate orthogonal.

Proof.

This first statement follows from
[
GGP11
, Thm 5.1(2)]
. By the paragraph just afterwards, independence follows whenever the parameter
\varphi_{v}
for
\pi_{v}
has conjugate orthogonal determinant. This is equivalent to the stated conditions by the discussion after Lemma
5.2.3
.
∎

Definition 3.3.5
.

If
N
is even or
\pi_{v}
is conjugate orthogonal, call
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})
for
\psi\circ\sigma=\psi^{-1}
as in
3.3.4
the standard root number of
\pi_{v}
.

Next, we recall much more difficult consequences of the endoscopic classification:

Theorem 3.3.6

(
[
Art13
, 1.5.3(b)]
)
.

Let
\pi
be a self-dual, orthogonal-type automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
. Then
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)=1
.

Proof.

Note that the trivial representation on
\mathrm{GL}_{1}
is orthogonal-type.
∎

Theorem 3.3.7

(
[
Mok15
, 2.5.4(b)]
)
.

Let
\pi
be a conjugate self-dual, conjugate orthogonal-type automorphic representation of
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}
. Then
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)=1
.

Proof.

The trivial representation on
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{1}
is conjugate orthogonal-type.
∎

3.4.
Involutions for Newforms : Self-Dual Case

We want to understand the functional equation (
3.2.3
) and the variant we need to compute epsilon factors for
\varphi
a newvector.
Reformulating
[
Tom24
, §4.2]
in adelic language, we define:

•

\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{p}^{k}
will as shorthand also be some chosen lifts of the corresponding ideals to
F_{v}^{\times}
or
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
determined by our choices of uniformizers
\varpi_{v}
. All terms subscripted with these ideals will depend on this choice.

•

J_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}:=J_{v,k}:=\diag(\varpi_{v}^{k},\cdots,\varpi_{v}^{k},1)\in\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})
.

•

J_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A})
for
\mathfrak{n}
an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
the analogous global product of
J_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}
.

•

\iota_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}=\iota_{v,k}:\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})\to\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F_{v}):g\mapsto g^{-T}J_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}
,

•

\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}:\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A})
the global version of
\iota_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}
,

•

\iota:=\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}
(consistent with the previous definition).

•

\varphi^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}:=\varphi\circ\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}
for global test vectors
\varphi
.

We also define an involution on local and global Whittaker models: if
w_{N}
is the long Weyl element as in (
2.2.1
), then

•

W^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{\varphi}(g):=W_{\varphi}(w_{N}g^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}):=W_{\varphi}(w_{N}g^{-T})
,

•

W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}(g):=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}g^{\iota_{v,k}}):=W_{\varphi}(w_{N}g^{-T}J_{v,k})
.

We justify this notation since globally, the left-invariance of
\varphi
under
G(F)
gives:

(3.4.1)

\displaystyle W_{\varphi^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}}(g)

\displaystyle=\int_{U(F)\backslash U(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(x^{-T}g^{-T}J_{\mathfrak{n}})\psi^{-1}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U(F)\backslash U(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(w_{N}x^{-T}g^{-T}J_{\mathfrak{n}})\psi^{-1}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U(F)\backslash U(\mathbb{A})}\varphi((w_{N}x^{-T}w_{N}^{-1})w_{N}g^{-T}J_{\mathfrak{n}})\psi^{-1}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U(F)\backslash U(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(xw_{N}g^{-T}J_{\mathfrak{n}})\psi^{-1}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=W^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{\varphi}(g).

We use here that the map
x\mapsto w_{N}x^{-T}w_{N}^{-1}
preserves
\psi
since

\displaystyle w_{N}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&\cdots&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1&x_{n-1}\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}^{-T}w_{N}^{-1}

\displaystyle=w_{N}\begin{pmatrix}1&-x_{1}&\cdots&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1&-x_{n-1}\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}^{T}w_{N}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{n-1}&\cdots&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1&x_{1}\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}.

It also preserves the Haar measure on
U
since it has finite order.

The same invariance of
\psi
gives that locally, if
x\in U(F_{v})
,

(3.4.2)

W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}(xg)=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}x^{-T}g^{-T}J_{v,k})=W_{\varphi_{v}}((w_{N}x^{-T}w_{N}^{-1})w_{N}g^{-T}J_{v,k})\\
=\psi(w_{N}x^{-T}w_{N}^{-1})W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}g^{-T}J_{v,k})=\psi(x)W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}(g).

so that
\iota_{v,k}
preserves the Gelfand-Graev representation. Next, we show that local Whittaker-newforms are actually eigenvectors of this involution:

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be a self-dual generic irreducible representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
of conductor
k
and
\varphi_{v}\in\pi_{v}
a newvector. Then
W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}=\pm W_{\varphi_{v}}
where both are in
W(\psi)
.

Proof.

By (
3.4.2
),
W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}
is in
W(\psi)
. Since
\iota_{v,k}
preserves invariance under right-translation by
K_{1,v}(k)
, we get that
W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}
is also fixed by
K_{1,v}(k)
. It generates a space isomorphic to
\pi_{v}^{\vee}\cong\pi_{v}
since for
h\in GL_{N,v}
,

W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{h\varphi_{v}}(g)=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}g^{-T}J_{v,k}h^{-1})=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}g^{-T}J_{v,k}h^{-1}J_{v,k}^{-1}J_{v,k})=\\
W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}(gJ^{-1}_{v,k}h^{T}J_{v,k})^{-T}J_{v,k})=W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}(gJ_{v,k}^{-1}h^{T}J_{v,k})=(J_{v,k}h^{-T}J_{v,k}^{-1})W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}(g).

In total, by uniqueness of Whittaker models and newvectors,
W_{\varphi_{v}}^{\iota_{v,k}}
has to be constant multiple of
W_{\varphi_{v}}
. Since
g\mapsto w_{N}g^{-T}J_{v,k}
is an involution, this multiple has to be
\pm 1
.
∎

Classically for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
, there is a link between the local eigenvalues from Lemma
3.4.1
and the root number coming from the use of global
\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}
in the proof of the functional equation for Hecke
L
-functions (e.g.
[
DS05
, 5.10.2]
). The correct generalization to
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
involves
N\times(N-1)
Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions. The standard functional equation only involves
\iota
; we give a variant for
\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}
:

Lemma 3.4.2
.

Let
\pi_{v},\pi^{\prime}_{v}
be generic, unitary irreducible representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
,
\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}
with central characters
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
respectively. Let
\varphi_{v},\varphi_{v}^{\prime}\in\pi_{v},\pi_{v}^{\prime}
respectively. Then

I_{v}(1-s,W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})\\
=\omega^{\prime}(-1)^{N-1}\omega^{\prime}(\varpi_{v}^{-k})q_{v}^{k(N-1)(s-1/2)}\gamma(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)I_{v}(s,W_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}).

Proof.

The local functional equation (
3.2.4
) gives:

I_{v}(1-s,W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})=\omega^{\prime}(-1)^{N-1}\gamma(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)I_{v}(s,W_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}).

However, using that
\varphi^{\iota}
has central character
\omega^{-1}
and that
\iota_{v,k}(g)=\iota(gJ^{-1}_{v,k})
:

\displaystyle I_{v}(s,W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{v}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}}\begin{pmatrix}\varpi_{v}^{-k}\mathbf{a}_{v}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}(\mathbf{a}_{v})|\det\mathbf{a}_{v}|^{s-1/2}\,d\mathbf{a}_{v}

\displaystyle=\omega^{\prime}(\varpi_{v}^{-k})q_{v}^{k(N-1)(1/2-s)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\int_{U_{v}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}}\begin{pmatrix}\varpi^{-k}\mathbf{a}_{v}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}(\varpi^{-k}\mathbf{a}_{v})|\det\varpi^{-k}\mathbf{a}_{v}|^{s-1/2}\,d(\varpi^{-k}\mathbf{a}_{v})

\displaystyle=\omega^{\prime}(\varpi_{v}^{-k})q_{v}^{k(N-1)(1/2-s)}\int_{U_{v}\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{a}_{v}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}(\mathbf{a}_{v})|\det\mathbf{a}_{v}|^{s-1/2}\,d\mathbf{a}_{v}

\displaystyle=\omega^{\prime}(\varpi_{v}^{-k})q_{v}^{k(N-1)(1/2-s)}I_{v}(s,W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}),

so the result follows.
∎

Next, we show that for
\pi_{v}
unramified the eigenvalue of
\iota
is always
1
:

Proposition 3.4.3
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be an unramified, self-dual generic irreducible representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
and
\varphi_{v}
an unramified vector. Then
W_{\varphi_{v}}=W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}}
independently of
\psi
.

Proof.

By the Iwasawa decomposition and since both sides of the equality are unramified, it suffices to check equality on elements of the form
u\lambda(\varpi)
for
u\in U_{N,v}
and
\lambda
a cocharacter of the diagonal torus. Here:

W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}}(u\lambda(\varpi))=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}u^{-T}\lambda(\varpi)^{-T})=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}u^{-T}w_{N}^{-1}\lambda^{\prime}(\varpi)w_{N})\\
=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}u^{-T}w_{N}^{-1}\lambda^{\prime}(\varpi)),

where if
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})
, then
\lambda^{\prime}:=-w_{N}\lambda w_{N}^{-1}=(-\lambda_{n},\dotsc,-\lambda_{1})
.

Factoring out the
u
terms, it therefore suffices to show that

\psi(u)W_{\varphi_{v}}(\lambda(\varpi))=\psi(w_{N}u^{-T}w_{N}^{-1})W_{\varphi_{v}}(\lambda^{\prime}(\varpi))\quad(=\psi(u)W_{\varphi_{v}}(\lambda^{\prime}(\varpi)).)

Since
\pi_{v}
is self-dual, its Satake parameter
(q_{v}^{\alpha_{1}},\dotsc,q_{v}^{\alpha_{N}})
is the same as the inverse
(q_{v}^{-\alpha_{N}},\dotsc,q_{v}^{-\alpha_{1}})
. The result therefore follows from the Casselman-Shalika formula (e.g.
[
HKP10
, Thm 6.5.1]
).
∎

Bootstrapping off this gives our key result:

Theorem 3.4.4
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be a generic self-dual representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
of conductor
k
. Let
\varphi_{v}\in\pi_{v}
be a newvector and let
\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})=\pm 1
be defined by
W_{\varphi_{v}}=\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}
as in Lemma
3.4.1
. Then

\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)^{N-1}.

If we further assume that
\pi_{v}
has trivial central character and
N
is even, then

\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})

and neither depend on the choice of
\psi
.

Proof.

Pick an auxilliary tempered, unramified, self-dual representation
\pi^{\prime}_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}
with Satake parameter
(q_{v}^{\alpha_{1}},\dotsc,q_{v}^{\alpha_{N-1}})
. Let
\varphi^{\prime}_{v}\in\pi^{\prime}_{v}
be the unramified vector. Normalize
\varphi_{v},\varphi^{\prime}_{v}
so that
W_{\varphi_{v}}(1)=W_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}(1)=1
. Then by Theorem
3.2.1
,

I_{v}(s,W_{\varphi},W_{\varphi^{\prime}})=L_{v}(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime}).

We can further assume that
\pi^{\prime}_{v}
has trivial central character. Then the definition of epsilon factors (
3.3.1
) and Lemma
3.4.2
give

I_{v}(s,W^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{\varphi_{v}},W^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})=\epsilon(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)q_{v}^{k(N-1)(s-1/2)}L_{v}(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v}).

Since
\pi^{\prime}_{v}
is self-dual,
W^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}=W_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}
by Proposition
3.4.3
. Together with Lemma
3.4.1
, this shows that

\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})I_{v}(s,W_{\varphi_{v}},W_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})=\epsilon(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)q_{v}^{k(N-1)(s-1/2)}L_{v}(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v})\\
\implies\epsilon(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)=\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}|\mathfrak{n}|^{(N-1)(1/2-s)}.

However, since
\pi^{\prime}
is unramified, we also have

L_{v}(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v})=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}L_{v}(s+\alpha_{i},\pi_{v}).

The definition of
\epsilon
-factors and multiplicativity of
\gamma
-factors (e.g.
[
Cog04
, §3.1.6]
) then gives:

\epsilon(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)=\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}\epsilon(s+\alpha_{i},\pi_{v},\psi)=\epsilon(s,\pi_{v},\psi)^{N-1}

using at the end that
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=0
since
\pi^{\prime}_{v}
is self-dual. This is the first claim.

If
\pi_{v}
is self-dual with trivial central character, by
3.3.3
and (
3.3.2
),

\epsilon(s,\pi_{v})=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})q_{v}^{k(1/2-s)}=\pm q_{v}^{k(1/2-s)}.

This implies that
\epsilon(1/2,s)=\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})
if
N-1
is odd.
∎

Note
.

The above theorem does not compute the local root number when
N
is odd or when
\pi_{v}
has central character. However, in those cases, any global
\pi
is orthogonal-type and therefore satisfies
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)=1
by Theorem
3.3.6
. Therefore this doesn’t matter for our eventual global application.

3.5.
Involutions for Newforms : Conjugate Self-Dual Case

We perform a similar argument for conjugate self-dual
\pi
. Let
E/F
be a quadratic extension with
\sigma:\alpha\mapsto\bar{\alpha}
the corresponding Galois conjugation action.

We fix our character
\psi:E^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
defining Whittaker functions to be self-conjugate, i.e.
\psi\circ\sigma=:\psi^{\sigma}=\psi
. It is very important to note that this is
not
the standard choice
\psi^{\sigma}=\psi^{-1}
used in
[
GGP11
]
. This annoying technical detail will add some extra central character factors that do not appear in the self-dual case.

For appropriate
v,\mathfrak{p}_{v},\mathfrak{n}
, we similarly define:

•

J_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}:=\diag(\varpi_{v}^{k},\cdots,\varpi_{v}^{k},1)\in\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})\subseteq\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
(note that if
v
is ramified, we need to instead choose a uniformizer
\varpi_{v}^{1/2}=\varpi_{w}
for
E_{w}
),

•

J_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{F})\subseteq\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{E})
for
\mathfrak{n}
an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
, the analogous global product of
J_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}
,

•

J_{v,k}
the locally-indexed version of
J_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}
, as in §
3.1.2
.

•

\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}:\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E):g\mapsto\bar{g}^{-T}J_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}
, and
\bar{\iota}_{v,k}
the locally-indexed version.

•

\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}:\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{E})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{A}_{E})
the global version of
\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}
.

•

\bar{\iota}:=\bar{\iota}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}

•

\varphi^{\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}}:=\varphi\circ\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}
for global test vectors
\varphi
.

We can also define for Whittaker functions:

•

W^{\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{\varphi}(g):=W_{\varphi}(w_{N}g^{\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}}):=W_{\varphi}(w_{N}\bar{g}^{-T})
,

•

W^{\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{\varphi_{v}}(g):=W_{\varphi_{v}}(w_{N}g^{\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}}):=W_{\varphi}(w_{N}\bar{g}^{-T}J_{v,k})
.

Following our choice of
\psi^{\sigma}=\psi
, this satisfies analogues of (
3.4.1
) and (
3.4.2
):

W_{\varphi^{\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{v}}=W^{\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{\varphi_{v}},\qquad W^{\bar{\iota}_{v,i}}_{\varphi_{v}}(xg)=\psi(x)W^{\bar{\iota}_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}(g).

We also warn that if
\varpi\neq\bar{\varpi}
, then
\bar{\iota}_{v,i}
isn’t necessarily an involution:

\bar{\iota}_{v,k}\bar{\iota}_{v,k}g=g\diag((\varpi/\overline{\varpi})^{-k},\dotsc,(\varpi/\overline{\varpi})^{-k},1).

However, this extra factor belongs to all level subgroups
K_{1,v}(k)
, so precomposition with
\bar{\iota}_{v,k}
does act as an involution on all new- and oldvector spaces.

Using all this, we continue the analogy:

Lemma 3.5.1
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be a generic conjugate self-dual irreducible representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
of conductor
k
and
\varphi_{v}\in\pi_{v}
a newvector. Then, provided that
\psi^{\sigma}=\psi
, we have
W^{\bar{\iota}_{v,k}}_{\psi,\varphi_{v}}=\pm W_{\psi,\varphi_{v}}
.

Proof.

This is a very similar argument to Lemma
3.4.1
, after noting that precomposition with
\bar{\iota}_{v,i}
does act as an involution on newvectors.
∎

For the functional equation:

Lemma 3.5.2
.

Assume
\psi^{\sigma}=\psi
. Let
\pi_{v},\pi^{\prime}_{v}
be generic, unitary irreducible representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E),\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
with central characters
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
respectively. Let
\varphi_{v},\varphi_{v}^{\prime}\in\pi_{v},\pi_{v}^{\prime}
respectively. Then

I_{v}(1-s,W^{\bar{\iota}_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\bar{\iota}}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})\\
=\omega^{\prime}(-1)^{N-1}\bar{\omega}^{\prime}(\overline{\varpi}_{v}^{-k}){q_{v}}^{k(N-1)(1/2-s)}\gamma(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)I_{v}(s,W_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}).

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.4.2
, we have that

I_{v}(s,W^{\bar{\iota}_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\bar{\iota}}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})=\bar{\omega}^{\prime}(\overline{\varpi}^{-k})q_{v}^{k(N-1)(1/2-s)}I_{v}(s,W^{\bar{\iota}}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\bar{\iota}}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})

noting that the
J_{v,k}
gets conjugated in the
\bar{\iota}_{v,k}
-version of the computation (Note also that this conjugation does nothing unless
v
is ramified and
k
is a half-integer). However, conjugation preserves Haar measures since it is an involution, so we also have that

I_{v}(s,W^{\bar{\iota}}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\bar{\iota}}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}})=I_{v}(s,W^{\iota}_{\varphi_{v}},\widehat{W}^{\iota}_{\varphi^{\prime}_{v}}).

The result follows as in Lemma
3.4.2
.
∎

Continuing with the analogous unramified input to
3.4.3
:

Proposition 3.5.3
.

Let
v
be non-split and
\pi_{v}
be an unramified, conjugate self-dual irreducible representation of
G_{N,v}=\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
and
\varphi_{v}
an unramified vector. Then
W_{\varphi_{v}}=W^{\bar{\iota}}_{\varphi_{v}}
whenever
\psi\circ\sigma=\psi
.

Proof.

This follows from
3.4.3
applied to
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(E_{w})
: for all
u\in U
,
\psi(\bar{u})=\psi(u)
and for all cocharacters
\lambda
,
W_{\varphi_{v}}(\lambda(\varpi))=W_{\varphi_{v}}(\lambda(\overline{\varpi}))
since
\varpi/\overline{\varpi}
has norm
1
. ∎

We now arrive at the conjugate self-dual theorem identifying
\bar{\tau}_{n}(\varphi_{v})
with the appropriate root number. This will enable us to construct test functions counting automorphic forms of conductor
\mathfrak{n}
weighted by their root number.

Theorem 3.5.4
.

Assume
\psi\circ\sigma=\psi
. Let
\pi_{v}
be a generic conjugate self-dual representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
of conductor
c(\pi_{v})=k
(as defined in §
3.1.2
). Let
\varphi_{v}\in\pi_{v}
be a newvector and let
\bar{\tau}^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})=\pm 1
be defined by
W_{\varphi_{v}}=\bar{\tau}^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})W^{\iota_{v,k}}_{\varphi_{v}}
as in Lemma
3.5.1
. Then

\bar{\tau}^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})=\begin{cases}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)^{N-1}&v\text{ non-split}\\
1&v\text{ split.}\end{cases}

Furthermore, in the non-split case, if either
N
is even or
\pi_{v}
is conjugate-orthogonal, let
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})
be the standard root number as in
3.3.5
. Then:

\bar{\tau}^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})=\begin{cases}\omega(a)\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})&N\text{ even}\\
1&N\text{ odd},\,\pi_{v}\text{ conjugate-orthogonal}\end{cases}

where
\omega
is the central character of
\pi
and
a\in F_{v}\otimes_{F}E
is any element such that
\sigma(a)=-a
.

Proof.

If
v
is split, then
\pi_{v}
is a representation of the form
\rho_{v}\boxtimes\rho_{v}^{\vee}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})^{2}
. If
W_{f}
is a newvector in the
\psi
-Whittaker model for
\rho_{v}
, then
W_{f}\boxtimes W^{\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}}_{f}
is a newvector for
\pi_{v}
. Since
\iota_{\mathfrak{n}}
acts as an involution,
\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}
acts as the identity on this newvector.

The non-split case follows very similarly to the proof of Theorem
3.4.4
. Explaining the details that change: We pick an auxiliary tempered, unramified, conjugate self-dual representation
\pi^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
with trivial central character and
\varphi^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}
. Using
3.5.2
/
3.5.3
instead of
3.4.2
/
3.4.3
, we get that

\epsilon(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v},\psi)=\bar{\tau}^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})q_{w}^{k(N-1)(1/2-s)}.

When
v
is non-split, we can also show by the same calculations as in
3.4.4
that

\epsilon(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime},\psi)=\epsilon(s,\pi_{v},\psi)^{N-1}.

Note that for unramified representations at non-split places
v
, conjugate self-dual is equivalent to self-dual since the valuation is unchanged by conjugation.

The second statement follows since

\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\pi,\psi)=\omega(a)\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{0})

and that we always have that
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{0})=\pm 1
and is independent of
\psi_{0}
under our conditions by
3.3.4
. We also note that
\omega(a)=\pm 1
when
\pi_{v}
is conjugate-orthogonal or
N
is even.
∎

Note
.

In the split case, let
\pi_{v}=\rho_{v}\boxtimes\rho_{v}^{\vee}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})^{2}
.
Then

\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)=\epsilon(1/2,\rho_{v},\psi)\epsilon(1/2,\rho_{v}^{\vee},\psi)=\omega_{\rho_{v}}(-1).

If
a\in F_{v}^{2}
such that
a=-\bar{a}
, then
a
is of the form
(x,-x)
and

\omega_{\pi}(a)=\omega_{\rho_{v}}(x)\omega_{\rho_{v}}(-x)^{-1}=\omega_{\rho_{v}}(-1).

In total
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(a)
. In other words, the value of
\bar{\tau}^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v})
for
v
split is
not

\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)^{N-1}
, but rather
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})^{N-1}:=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{0})^{N-1}
.

In particular, the split case differs from the non-split case by a factor of
\omega(a)^{N-1}
.

Note
.

It is at this point that the restriction to
N
even in our main Theorem
10.3.2
appears. In the self-dual case, we get lucky that when
N
is odd, all representations are orthogonal-type and therefore have global root number
1
. Here, however, there are conjugate-symplectic-type representations when
N
is odd which can have arbitrary root number. Our techniques are unable to address this case.

4.
Test Functions

Using the results of the previous section, we now construct test functions that let us compute (twisted) counts of representations.

4.1.
Twisted Action

Recall from (
2.2.1
) the matrix
w_{N}
which conjugated
\iota,\bar{\iota}
into the involutions
\theta,\bar{\theta}
preserving the standard pinning. We define twisted groups:

\widetilde{G}_{N}=\begin{cases}G_{N}\rtimes\theta&\text{self-dual or orthogonal/symplectic case}\\
G_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta}&\text{conjugate self-dual or unitary case.}\end{cases}

We first realize our involutions
\iota_{\mathfrak{n}},\bar{\iota}_{\mathfrak{n}}
through conjugation in the twisted group
\widetilde{G}_{N}
.

If
\pi
is a (conjugate) self-dual generic automorphic representation of
G_{N}
, the twisted group
\widetilde{G}_{N}
acts on
\pi
since
\theta
(or
\bar{\theta}
) acts on its Whittaker model through precomposition. Define
\widetilde{\pi}
to be this extended representation of
\widetilde{G}_{N}
. This lets us define twisted traces
\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}}\tilde{f}
for test functions
\tilde{f}
on
\widetilde{G}_{N}
. Note that this all depends on the choice of
\psi
used to define the Whittaker model.

Note
.

In the conjugate self-dual case, this choice of
\psi
is a priori different than in the previous section. Specifically: before, we were thinking of the local
G_{N,v}
as
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
and defining Whittaker models through characters
\psi
of
F_{v}\otimes_{F}E
. Now, we are working with the Whittaker models of the algebraic group
G_{N,v}/F_{v}
depending on a choice of character
\psi
of
F_{v}
. This choice in turn determines a choice of character of
F_{v}\otimes_{F}E
through composition with the trace map and only produces characters of
F_{v}\otimes_{F}E
satisfying
\psi^{\sigma}=\psi
, as was required in §
3.5
.

Since each of the
\pi_{v}
are also generic, each
\widetilde{G}_{N,v}
also acts on
\pi_{v}
through precomposition in the Whittaker model. This defines local twisted traces
\tr_{\tilde{\pi}_{v}}\tilde{f}_{v}
for test functions
\tilde{f}_{v}
on
\widetilde{G}_{N,v}
. Then, as is well-known:

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Let
\tilde{f}=\prod_{v}\tilde{f}_{v}
be a test function on
\widetilde{G}_{N}
. Then for all generic automorphic representations
\pi
of
G_{N}

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}}\tilde{f}=\prod_{v}\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}}\tilde{f}_{v}.

Proof.

Through the isomorphism of
G(\mathbb{A})
-reps from
\pi
to its global Whittaker model and computation (
3.4.1
), we can calculate
\tr_{\tilde{\pi}}\tilde{f}
as the trace of a convolution operator on the global Whittaker model and then factor.
∎

Our consideration of twisted traces is motivated by the multiplication formulas:

(4.1.1)

\begin{cases}(J^{-1}_{v,k}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)W_{\varphi}=W_{\varphi_{v}}^{\iota_{v,k}}&\text{self-dual},\\
(J^{-1}_{v,k}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})W_{\varphi}=W_{\varphi_{v}}^{\bar{\iota}_{v,k}}&\text{conjugate self-dual.}\end{cases}

—i.e. we can relate the eigenvalue of the appropriate
\iota
on a newvector to traces against
Jw_{N}\rtimes\theta
.

In particular, if we define

\tilde{f}_{v,k}=(J^{-1}_{v,k}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K_{1,v(k)}},

and assume that
\pi_{v}
has conductor
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}
with newvector
\varphi_{v}
, we get:

\tr_{\tilde{\pi}_{v}}\tilde{f}_{v,k}=\tr_{\tilde{\pi}_{v}^{K_{1,v}(k)}}(\iota_{v,k})=\tau^{\psi}_{v,k}(\varphi_{v}).

from
3.4.4
. Therefore,
\tilde{f}_{v,k}
works as our test function to probe
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})
if
\pi_{v}
has conductor exactly
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}
. However, it also picks up the traces against oldform spaces for
\pi_{v}
of conductor less than
k
.

4.2.
Twisted Traces on Spaces of Oldforms

We now study the extra oldform contributions.

4.2.1.
Description of the Oldform Space

We first recall results from
[
Ree91
]
describing the space of
K_{1,v}(k)
