-fixed vectors in any generic representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
in terms of a Hecke algebra for
GL_{N-1,v}
. This lets us compute the action of
J_{v,i}w_{N}\rtimes\theta
on all oldforms instead of just the newvectors.

Let
\mathscr{H}_{N-1}
be the unramified (with respect to
K=\mathrm{GL}_{N-1}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
) Hecke algebra for
\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}
. Let
T\subseteq\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
be the diagonal torus so that
\mathscr{H}_{N-1}
has a Cartan basis of double coset indicator functions
\mathbf{1}_{K\lambda(\varpi_{v})K}
for
\lambda
a dominant cocharacter of
T
.

Every such
\lambda
is a unique linear combination of the fundamental coweights

\lambda_{i}:=(\overbrace{1,\dotsc,1}^{i},\overbrace{0,\dotsc,0}^{N-1-i})\qquad 1\leq i\leq N-1

with all coefficients positive except for that of
\lambda_{N-1}
. For
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N-1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, define

T(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N-1}):=\mathbf{1}_{K(a_{1}\lambda_{1}+\cdots+a_{N-1}\lambda_{N-1})(\varpi_{v})K}\in\mathscr{H}_{N-1}

and

\mathscr{H}_{N-1}^{\leq k}:=\left\langle T(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N-1}):a_{i}\geq 0,\sum a_{i}\leq k\right\rangle\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{N-1}.

There is a normalized action of
\mathscr{H}_{N-1}
on
\pi_{v}
given by

h*\varphi:=\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}h(g)\begin{pmatrix}g^{-1}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\varphi|\det g|^{1/2}\,dg.

Theorem 4.2.1

(Slight modification of
[
Ree91
, Thm. 1]
)
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be a generic irreducible representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})
with conductor
c
and newvector
\varphi_{v}
. Then for
k\geq 0
, the map

h\mapsto h*\varphi_{v}

induces a linear isomorphism

\Psi_{\pi_{v}}:\mathscr{H}_{N-1}^{\leq k}\to\pi_{v}^{K_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{c+k})}.

Proof.

The Satake transform lets us change between the basis for
\mathscr{H}_{N-1}^{\leq k}
described here and the one in
[
Ree91
]
.
∎

4.2.2.
Twisted Action on Oldvectors: Self-Dual Case

Using the isomorphism
\Psi_{\pi_{v}}
, we can compute a key formula the self-dual case:

Proposition 4.2.2
.

Fix a generic self-dual representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
with conductor
c
and newvector
\varphi_{v}\in\pi_{v}
. Then we have

(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\Psi_{\pi_{v}}(h|\det|^{-1/2})=\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})\Psi_{\pi_{v}}(h(\varpi_{v}^{k}(*)^{-T})|\det|^{-1/2})

for all
h\in\mathscr{H}_{N-1}
and where we recall
\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})=\pm 1
is the eigenvalue of
(J_{v,c}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)
acting on
\varphi_{v}
.

Proof.

Computing and recalling that
\Psi_{\pi_{v}}(\varphi)=h*\varphi_{v}
:

\displaystyle(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\Psi_{\pi_{v}}(h|\det|^{-1/2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}h(g)(J_{v,k+c}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\begin{pmatrix}g^{-1}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\varphi_{v}\,dg

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}h(g)\begin{pmatrix}g^{T}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}(J_{v,k+c}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\varphi_{v}\,dg

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}h(g)\begin{pmatrix}g^{T}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}J_{v,k}^{-1}(J_{v,c}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\varphi_{v}\,dg

\displaystyle=\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}h(g)\begin{pmatrix}\varpi_{v}^{-k}g^{T}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\varphi_{v}\,dg

\displaystyle=\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})\int_{\mathrm{GL}_{N-1,v}}h(\varpi_{v}^{k}g^{-T})\begin{pmatrix}g^{-1}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\varphi_{v}\,dg

\displaystyle=\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})\Psi_{\pi_{v}}(h(\varpi_{v}^{k}(*)^{-T})|\det|^{-1/2}),

which finishes the argument.
∎

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Fix a generic self-dual representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
with conductor
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{c}
, and newvector
\varphi_{v}
. Then,

•

If
N
is even:

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}^{K_{1}(c+k)}}(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)=\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})\begin{cases}\binom{k/2+N/2-1}{N/2-1}&k\text{ even}\\
0&k\text{ odd.}\end{cases}

•

If
N
is odd:

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}^{K_{1}(c+k)}}(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)=\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})\begin{cases}\binom{k/2+(N-1)/2}{(N-1)/2}&k\text{ even}\\
\binom{(k-1)/2+(N-1)/2}{(N-1)/2}&k\text{ odd.}\end{cases}

We recall
\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})=\pm 1
is the eigenvalue of
(J_{v,c}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)
acting on
\varphi_{v}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.2.2
and Theorem
4.2.1
, it suffices to compute the trace of

h|\det|^{-1/2}\mapsto h(\varpi_{v}^{k}(*)^{-T})|\det|^{-1/2}

on
\mathscr{H}_{N-1}^{\leq k}
. We use the basis of elements
T(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N-1})|\det|^{-1/2}
.

Since
-\lambda_{i}
is Weyl-conjugate to
\lambda_{N-1-i}-\lambda_{N-1}
for
1\leq i\leq N-2
, we get that if
\sum a_{i}=d
,

(4.2.1)

T(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N-1})(\varpi_{v}^{k}g^{-T})=T(a_{N-2},a_{N-3},\cdots,a_{1},k-d)(g).

The trace is then exactly the number fixed points of this involution: indices
(a_{i})_{i}
such that

(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N-1})=(a_{N-2},a_{N-3},\cdots,a_{1},k-d).

If
N-2
is even, there are only fixed points when
k
is even. In this case, the choices for
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N/2-1}
determine the fixed point and can be any values whose sum is
\leq k/2
. This gives the claimed formula.

If
N-2
is odd, the choices for
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{(N-1)/2}
still determine the fixed point. If
k
is even, then
a_{(N-1)/2}
is even and the rest can be any values whose sum is
\leq k-a_{(N-1)/2}
. If
k
is odd, then the situation is the same except
a_{(N-1)/2}
needs to be odd. Both case give the claimed formula.
∎

4.2.3.
Twisted Action on Oldvectors: Conjugate Self-Dual Case

Similarly:

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Fix a generic conjugate self-dual representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
with conductor
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{c}
and newvector
\varphi_{v}
. Then we have

(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})\Psi_{\pi_{v}}(h|\det|^{-1/2})=\bar{\tau}_{c}(\varphi_{v})\Psi_{\pi_{v}}(h(\varpi_{v}^{k}\bar{(*)}^{-T})|\det|^{-1/2})

for all
h\in\mathscr{H}_{N-1}
and where we recall
\bar{\tau}_{c}(\varphi_{v})=\pm 1
is the eigenvalue of
(J_{v,c}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})
acting on
\varphi_{v}
.

Proof.

This is the same argument as Proposition
4.2.2
after noting that conjugation sends any
K_{N-1,v}
-double coset to itself.
∎

Proposition 4.2.5
.

Fix a generic conjugate self-dual representation
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)
with (locally-indexed as in §
3.1.2
) conductor
c(\pi_{v})=k
and newvector
\varphi_{v}
. Then,

•

If
v
is non-split and
N
is even:

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}^{K_{1}(c+k)}}(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})=\bar{\tau}_{c}(\varphi_{v})\begin{cases}\binom{k/2+N/2-1}{N/2-1}&k\text{ even}\\
0&k\text{ odd}\end{cases}

•

If
v
non-split and
N
is odd:

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}^{K_{1}(c+k)}}(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})=\bar{\tau}_{c}(\varphi_{v})\begin{cases}\binom{k/2+(N-1)/2}{(N-1)/2}&k\text{ even}\\
\binom{(k-1)/2+(N-1)/2}{(N-1)/2}&k\text{ odd}\end{cases}

•

If
v
is split:

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}^{K_{1}(c+k)}}(J_{v,c+k}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})=\bar{\tau}_{c}(\varphi_{v})\binom{k+N-1}{N-1}.

We recall
\tau_{c}(\varphi_{v})=\pm 1
is the eigenvalue of
(J_{v,c}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})
acting on
\varphi_{v}
.

Proof.

For the non-split cases,
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)=\mathrm{GL}_{N}(E_{w})
for some
w|v
. The basis elements
T(a_{1},\dotsc,a_{N-1})
defined via the analogous uniformizer
\varpi_{w}
of
\mathcal{O}_{w}
are fixed under conjugation. Therefore, these two cases follow from Proposition
4.2.3
applied to representations of
GL_{N}(E_{w})
.

For the split case,
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)=\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})^{2}
so the relevant version of
\mathscr{H}_{N-1}^{\leq k}
is the product of two copies of the version from
4.2.3
, where conjugation switches the two factors. There is then exactly one fixed point of the relevant action from
4.2.4
for each choice of the first factor.
∎

4.3.
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
: Counts Weighted by Epsilon Factors

Next, we find a twisted trace that counts representations
\pi
of level
\mathfrak{n}
weighted by their root numbers
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)
.

4.3.1.
Local Test Function

Working locally, Propositions
4.2.2
and
4.2.4
let us solve an upper-triangular system of linear equations to produce the local factors of the test function giving weighted counts of automorphic forms:

Corollary 4.3.1
.

Consider

G_{N,v}
in either the self-dual or conjugate self-dual case—i.e.
G_{v}=\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
or
\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
for
v
split, ramified or inert. Then there are coefficients
a_{N}(k,i)
such that if we define

\widetilde{E}_{v,k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}a_{N}(k,i)(J_{v,k-i}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K_{1,v}(k-i)},

then for
\pi_{v}
a generic representation of
G_{v}
we have

\tr_{\pi_{v}}(\widetilde{E}_{k,v})=\begin{cases}\tau&c(\pi_{v})=k\\
0&\text{else},\end{cases}

where
\tau
is the eigenvalue of
\iota_{v,k}
or
\bar{\iota}_{v,k}
acting on the newvector for
\pi_{v}
.

We can compute
a_{N}(k,i)
exactly in many cases:

Proposition 4.3.2
.

The following formulas hold for
\widetilde{E}_{v,k}
from Corollary
4.3.1
::

•

self-dual case,
N
even,

\widetilde{E}_{v,k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{N/2}(-1)^{i}\binom{N/2}{i}(J_{v,k-2i}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\theta)\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K_{1,v}(k-2i)},

•

conjugate self-dual case,
N
even,
v
non-split:

\widetilde{E}_{v,k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{N/2}(-1)^{i}\binom{N/2}{i}(J_{v,k-2i}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K_{1,v}(k-2i)},

•

conjugate self-dual case,
v
split

\widetilde{E}_{v,k}:=\sum_{i=0}^{N}(-1)^{i}\binom{N}{i}(J_{v,k-i}^{-1}w_{N}\rtimes\bar{\theta})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K_{1,v}(k-i)}.

Proof.

This follows from the formulas in Propositions
4.2.2
and
4.2.4
and the identity

\sum_{i=0}^{b+1}(-1)^{i}\binom{b+1}{i}\binom{a-i+b}{b}=\begin{cases}1&a=0\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases},

for
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. The combinatorial identity in turn follows from the simpler identity
\sum_{i=0}^{b+1}(-1)^{i}\binom{b+1}{i}i^{k}=0
when
k<b+1
, as first proved by Euler (see
[
Gou78
]
).

See also
[
Bin17
]
for a similar version of this result for untwisted traces.
∎

4.3.2.
Global Test Function

Putting our local test functions globally:

Definition 4.3.3
.

In the self-dual case, if
\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{v}\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}
is an ideal of
F
, define the test function

\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\prod_{v}\widetilde{E}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}}

on
\widetilde{G}_{N}(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})
in terms of the
\widetilde{E}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}}
from Corollary
4.3.1
.

In the conjugate-self dual case, we need to twist by a central character at non-split places to deal with the corresponding discrepancy in
3.5.4
:

Definition 4.3.4
.

In the conjugate self-dual case, let
\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{v}\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}
be a conductor as in §
3.1.2
. Choose
a\in E^{\times}
with
\bar{a}=-a
(by Hilbert 90) and define the test function

\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\prod_{v\text{ split}}\widetilde{E}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}}\prod_{v\text{ ns.}}\delta_{a^{N-1}}\star\widetilde{E}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}}

on
\widetilde{G}_{N}(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})
in terms of the
\widetilde{E}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}}
from Corollary
4.3.1
(but now globally indexed).

Theorem 4.3.5
.

Consider the self-dual case
G_{N}=\mathrm{GL}_{N}/F
. Choose
\mathfrak{n}
an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Then for all generic self-dual automorphic representations
\pi
of
G_{N}
:

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}^{\infty}}(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=\begin{cases}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty})^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n},\pi\text{ symplectic-type}\\
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty},\psi)^{-N+1}&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n},\pi\text{ orthogonal-type}\\
0&c(\pi)\neq\mathfrak{n}\end{cases}

(where the second case may depend on the choice of
\psi
made to define
\widetilde{\pi}
).

Proof.

The
c(\pi)\neq\mathfrak{n}
case follows from multiplying together the same cases of Corollary
4.3.1
.

If
c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}
, then let
\varphi=\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\varphi_{v}
be in the space of newvectors for
\pi^{\infty}
. Then by Proposition
4.1.1
, Corollary
4.3.1
, and (
4.1.1
),

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}^{\infty}}(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}}(\widetilde{E}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}})=\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\tau_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}(\varphi_{v}).

If
\pi
symplectic-type, then
N
is even and
\pi
has trivial central character so Theorem
3.4.4
reduces this to

\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty})^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)

which is independent of
\psi
. The result follows.

In general, the trace is
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty},\psi)^{-N+1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi,\psi)^{N-1}
. However, when
\pi
is orthogonal-type,
[
Art13
, Thm 1.5.3(b)]
gives that
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)=\epsilon(1/2,\pi\times\mathrm{triv})=1
.
∎

For the conjugate self-dual case, we need to choose
\psi=\psi\circ\sigma
so we can understand the action of
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
on newvectors using
3.5.4
:

Theorem 4.3.6
.

Consider the conjugate self-dual case
G_{N}=\Res^{E}_{F}\mathrm{GL}_{N}
and define extensions of representations to
\widetilde{G}_{N}
using
\psi
such that
\psi\circ\sigma=\psi
. Choose
\mathfrak{n}
a conjugate self-dual conductor (as in
3.1.2
). Then for all generic conjugate self-dual automorphic representations
\pi
of
G_{N}
with central character
\omega
:

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}^{\infty}}(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=\begin{cases}\omega_{\infty}(a)^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty})^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n},N\text{ even},\;\pi\text{ conj.-symp.}\\
\omega_{\infty}(a)^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty})^{-1}&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n},N\text{ even},\;\pi\text{ conj.-orth.}\\
\omega_{\infty}(a)^{-N+1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty},\psi)^{-N+1}&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n},N\text{ odd},\;\pi\text{ conj.-symp.}\\
1&c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n},N\text{ odd},\;\pi\text{ conj.-orth.}\\
0&c(\pi)\neq\mathfrak{n}\end{cases}

where
a
appears in the definition of
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Note that the
\epsilon
-terms without
\psi
are standard root numbers with respect to some local
\psi_{0}\circ\sigma=\psi_{0}^{-1}
as in
3.3.4
.

Proof.

Similar to Theorem
4.3.5
, the trace is
0
unless
c(\pi)=\mathfrak{n}
in which case

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}^{\infty}}(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})=\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\bar{\tau}_{v,\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}}(\varphi_{v}).

Recall the choice of
a\in E^{\times}
such that
\bar{a}=-a
to define
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
. We use
3.5.4
with this choice of
a
at all places:

In the first and second cases, the central character is conjugate-orthogonal and therefore valued in
\pm 1
so
\omega^{N-1}=\omega
. Therefore, the trace is

\omega_{\mathrm{spl}}(a)^{N-1}\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\nmid\infty\\
v\text{ n.s.}\end{subarray}}\omega_{v}(a)=\omega_{\infty}(a)^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty})^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi),

using the note after
3.5.4
at split places. For the second case, we additionally use Theorem
3.3.7
to compute that
\epsilon(1/2,\pi)=1
globally.

The third case is similar to the first: the trace is equal to

\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{\infty},\psi)^{-N+1}\omega_{\infty}(a)^{-N+1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi)^{N-1}.

Since the global root number is independent of
\psi
, multiplying together
3.3.4
gives that it is
\pm 1
even for our non-standard
\psi
. Therefore, the third factor vanishes.

The fourth case follows immediately from the corresponding case of
3.5.4
.
∎

Note
.

If
\pi
is unramified at a split place
v
, then it can be shown that
\omega_{v}(a)=1
. Therefore, if
\pi
is unramified at all split places,
\omega_{\mathrm{ns}}(a)=\omega_{\infty}(a)^{-1}
.

Beware again that for
N
odd, the test function
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
fails to produce any information about root numbers.

4.4.
\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
: Unweighted Counts through a Twisted Trace

We now use the twisted trace-Paley-Weiner theorem of
[
Rog88
]
to modify
\widetilde{E}_{v,k}
into a function
\widetilde{C}_{v,k}
on
\widetilde{G}_{N,v}
counting representations of conductor
\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k}
unweighted by their root number.

As the Paley-Weiner input:

Proposition 4.4.1
.

Let
\widetilde{G}_{v}=G_{v}\rtimes\theta
be a twisted group and
\widetilde{f}_{v}
a test function on
\widetilde{G}_{v}
. Choose a constant
a_{\Theta}
for each Bernstein component
\Theta
of
G_{v}
. Then there exists a test function
\widetilde{f}_{v}^{\prime}
on
\widetilde{G}_{v}
such that if
\widetilde{\pi}_{v}
is an extension to
\widetilde{G}_{v}
of the self-dual irreducible representation
\pi_{v}\in\Theta
, we have

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}}\widetilde{f}_{v}^{\prime}=a_{\Theta}\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}}\widetilde{f}_{v}.

Proof.

This is a special case of the main theorem of
[
Rog88
]
taking in to account the characterization of twisted Bernstein components in §6 therein. Note that self-dual is the same as
\theta
-stable.
∎

In summary, a local twisted test function
can be modified by rescaling it by a different constant
a_{\Theta}
on each Bernstein component. We next see that root numbers are constant on representations of a given conductor belonging to a specific
\Theta
.

Proposition 4.4.2
.

In the self-dual case, let
\Theta
be a Bernstein component of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
. Choose
k\geq 0
. Then there is a constant
C_{\Theta,k}
such that for all self-dual irreducible representations
\pi_{v}\in\Theta
with conductor
c(\pi_{v})=k
, we have
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})=C_{\Theta,k}
.

Proof.

Fix a product of supercuspidals
\boxtimes_{i\in I}\rho_{i}
on
\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}
with
\sum n_{i}=N
that determines a Bernstein component
\Theta
. Possibly twisting by a power of
|\det|
on each factor, which leaves
\Theta
unchanged, we assume that for all pairs
(i,j)
, there is no
s\neq 0
such that
\rho_{i}|\det|^{s}=\rho_{j}
.

Now consider a self-dual
\pi_{v}\in\Theta
, i.e.
\pi_{v}
is a subquotient of the parabolic induction of
\boxtimes_{i\in I}\rho_{i}|\det|^{s_{i}}
for some
s_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

By the Bernstein-Zelevinsky classification
[
BZ77
]
, there is a partition of the sequence
(\rho_{i}|\det|^{s_{i}})_{i}
into segments
\delta_{j}
of the form

\delta_{j}=(\sigma_{j}|\det|^{r_{j}+\frac{1}{2}(t_{j}-1)},\sigma_{j}|\det|^{r_{j}+\frac{1}{2}(t_{j}-3)},\dotsc,\sigma_{j}|\det|^{r_{j}-\frac{1}{2}(t_{j}-1)})

such that if
\Delta_{j}
is the discrete representation of
GL_{n_{j}t_{j}}
associated to the segment
\delta_{j}
, then
\pi_{v}
is the Langlands quotient of
\boxtimes_{j}\Delta_{j}
. Our condition on the
\rho_{i}
lets us assume, up to making the correct choice of
r_{j}
, that the multiset of
t_{j}
copies of each
\sigma_{j}
is the same as the multiset of
\rho_{i}
’s.

By
[
Hen86
, p. 153, (2)]
, the root number of
\pi_{v}
satisfies

(4.4.1)

\epsilon(s,\pi_{v})=\prod_{j}\epsilon(s,\Delta_{j}).

Furthermore, if
\sigma_{j}
is ramified, then recalling that ramified supercuspidals on
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
have trivial
L
-factor, we have by
[
Hen86
, p. 153, (5)]
:

(4.4.2)

\epsilon(s,\Delta_{j})=\prod_{k=1}^{t_{j}}\epsilon(s,\sigma_{j}|\det|^{r_{j}+\frac{1}{2}(t_{j}+1)-k})\\
=\prod_{k=1}^{t_{j}}\epsilon(s,\sigma_{j})q_{v}^{c_{\sigma_{j}}(r_{j}+\frac{1}{2}(t_{j}+1)-k)}=\epsilon(s,\sigma_{j})^{t_{j}}q_{v}^{c_{\sigma_{j}}r_{j}t_{j}}.

If
\sigma_{j}
is unramified, then it is necessarily of the form
|\det|^{s}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{1}
and
\Delta_{j}
is of the form
\mathrm{St}(t_{j})|\det|^{r_{j}}
where
\mathrm{St}(t_{j})
is the Steinberg representation on
\mathrm{GL}_{t_{j}}
. Since
\mathrm{St}(t_{j})
has conductor
t_{j}-1
and root number
(-1)^{t_{j}-1}
by, e.g,
[
Tat79
, (4.1.4)-(4.1.6)]
, we get

(4.4.3)

\epsilon(s,\Delta_{j})=\epsilon(s,\mathrm{St}(t_{j}))q_{v}^{(t_{j}-1)r_{j}}=(-1)^{t_{j}-1}q_{v}^{(t_{j}-1)(1/2-s)}q_{v}^{(t_{j}-1)r_{j}}.

Putting the formulas (
4.4.1
)—(
4.4.3
) together, let
J_{0}\subseteq J
be the set of indices
j
such that
\Delta_{j}
is a Steinberg (also define
I_{0}\subseteq I
correspondingly). Then we can compute the conductor

(4.4.4)

c(\pi_{v})=\sum_{j\in J_{0}}(t_{j}-1)+\sum_{j\in J\setminus J_{0}}t_{j}c_{\sigma_{j}}=u-u_{0}+\sum_{i\in I}c_{\rho_{i}}

for
u
the total number of unramified
\rho_{i}
(equivalently, of unramified
\sigma_{j}
) and
u_{0}
the total number of
\Delta_{j}
that are Steinberg so that
u-u_{0}=\sum_{j\in J_{0}}(t_{j}-1)
.

Also, since
\pi_{v}
is self-dual, there is a decomposition of the indexing set

J=J_{d}\sqcup J_{n}

with
J_{d}
consisting of indices such that
\sigma_{j}
is self-dual and
r_{j}=0
, and
J_{n}
consisting of pairs of indices
j,j^{\prime}
such that
r_{j}=-r_{j^{\prime}}
and
\sigma_{j}=\sigma^{\vee}_{j^{\prime}}
(in particular
c_{\sigma_{j}}=c_{\sigma^{\vee}_{j^{\prime}}})
.
Therefore, when multiplying (
4.4.2
) and (
4.4.3
) over all
j
, all the powers of
q_{v}^{r_{j}}
cancel out, leaving:

\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v})=\prod_{j\in J_{0}}(-1)^{t_{j}-1}\prod_{j\in J\setminus J_{0}}\epsilon(1/2,\sigma_{j})^{t_{j}}=(-1)^{u-u_{0}}\prod_{i\in I\setminus I_{0}}\epsilon(1/2,\rho_{i})=\\
=(-1)^{u-u_{0}}\prod_{i\in I}\epsilon(1/2,\rho_{i}).

Finally, by
4.4.4
, this becomes

(-1)^{c_{\pi}-\sum_{i\in I}c_{\rho_{i}}}\prod_{i\in I}\epsilon(1/2,\rho_{i}),

which is our
C_{\Theta,c(\pi_{v})}
that only depends on the
\rho_{i}
and
c(\pi_{v})
.
∎

Proposition 4.4.3
.

Fix a character
\psi
to define local
\epsilon
-factors. In the conjugate self-dual case, let
\Theta
be a Bernstein component of
\mathrm{GL}_{N,v}
for
v
non-split. Choose
k\geq 0
. Then there is constant
C_{\Theta,k}
such that for conjugate self-dual irreducible representations
\pi_{v}\in\Theta
with conductor
c(\pi_{v})=k
, we have
\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi)=C_{\Theta,k}
.

Proof.

This is the same argument as Proposition
4.4.2
since invariance of the
v
-adic norm under conjugation means that conjugate-self duality forces the same pairing condition on the
r_{j}
.
∎

Now we are ready to define the local components of our test function that gives an unweighted count of representations:

Corollary 4.4.4
.

In either the self-dual or conjugate self dual case, there is a test function
\widetilde{C}_{v,k}
on
\widetilde{G}_{N,v}
such that for all generic (conjugate) self-dual
\pi_{v}

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}}\widetilde{C}_{v,k}=\begin{cases}1&c(\pi_{v})=k\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}.

Proof.

For all
\pi_{v}
in Bernstein components
\Theta
such that
\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}_{v}}\widetilde{E}_{v,k}\neq 0
, we have that
C_{\Theta,k}^{-(N-1)}
from Proposition
4.4.2
(resp.
4.4.3
) is the same as the
\tau
from Corollary
4.3.1
by Proposition
3.4.4
(resp.
3.5.4
). Therefore, we just apply Proposition
4.4.1
to
\widetilde{E}_{k,v}
with constants
a_{\Theta}=C_{\Theta,k}^{-(N-1)}
.
∎

Putting these together globally:

Definition 4.4.5
.

If
\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{v}\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}
is an ideal of
F
, define the test function on
\widetilde{G}_{N}(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})
:

\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\prod_{v}\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}}

in terms of the
\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{k_{v}}}
from Corollary
4.4.4
(but now globally indexed) in either the self-dual or conjugate self dual case.

This of course satisfies:

Corollary 4.4.6
.

In either the conjugate self-dual or self-dual case, for all generic self-dual representations
\pi^{\infty}
of
(G_{N})^{\infty}
,

\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}^{\infty}}\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\begin{cases}1&c(\pi^{\infty})=\mathfrak{n}\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Note
.

Unlike the case of
\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
(specifically against orthogonal or conjugate-orthogonal representations), the trace identity characterizing
\widetilde{C}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}}
applies to more general irreducible representations of
(G_{N})^{\infty}
than just the ones that are factors of automorphic representations. This will be important for our spectral argument to understand its transfers in Section
9
.

5.
Asymptotics for the Cuspidal Spectrum: Setup

To compute our counts, we need to isolate certain parts of the automorphic spectrum on
G_{N}
through the trace formula:

•

In the self-dual or orthogonal/symplectic case, the cuspidal, self-dual representations of either orthogonal or symplectic-type with a given infinitesimal character
\lambda
at infinity.

•

In the conjugate self-dual or unitary case, the cuspidal, conjugate self-dual representations with a given infinitesimal character
\lambda
at infinity.

We do this using the endoscopic classification through the formalism of refined shapes of parameters from
[
DGG22
, §4,5]
. While the characterization of the discrete spectrum of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
in
[
MW89
]
might appear to be enough on the spectral side, giving a tractable geometric-side expression requires the full methods of
[
DGG22
]
.

We freely use notation analogous to
[
DGG22
]
throughout. In the case of
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2N}^{\eta}
, we will suppress the bars from
[
Art13
]
that represent orbits under the outer automorphism and take the unbarred notations to represent these orbits. The
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2N}^{\eta}
case is not a main focus of this paper and this change is irrelevant for other
G
.

Recall also the list of simple endoscopic groups of
\widetilde{G}_{N}
from §
2.2.2
and assumption
2.3.3
on our number fields.

Note
.

A comment on the use of the endoscopic classification and the induction of
[
Taï17
]
: First, we use the it to separate globally symplectic-type and orthogonal-type automorphic representations as this distinction cannot even be defined otherwise. An alternate method is using a working sufficient condition that if
\pi
has local component
\pi_{v}
with only a single symmetry type, then
\pi
has that same symmetry type. Through this, we can pick out some representations with fixed symmetry type using just the twisted trace formula as in
[
TW24
]
.

However, while the purely twisted-trace-formula strategy is conceptually simpler, the lack of a twisted analogue of the geometric expansion of
[
Art89
]
makes it much more analytically and computationally involved. In our case, we trade-off the abstract complexity of the inductive analysis of
[
Taï17
]
to make the explicit computations at the bottom tractable, particularly for our very complicated test functions. This also allows inputting the much stronger error-term bounds of
[
ST16
]
.

5.1.
Infinitesimal characters

Recall that the infinitesimal character
\lambda
is an invariant of irreducible representations of a reductive Lie group
G(\mathbb{R})
. We have
\lambda\in\Omega_{G}\backslash\Hom(\mathfrak{t},\mathbb{C})
, where
\mathfrak{t}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
G(\mathbb{R})
. This data is the same as a map from Weyl orbits of
X^{*}(\widehat{\mathfrak{t}})=X_{*}(\mathfrak{t})\to\mathbb{C}
, which is further the same as a semisimple conjugacy class in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
.

5.1.1.
Infinitesimal characters in the classification

We need a uniform way to talk about infinitesimal characters at infinity for all
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
.

In the self-dual case, the embedding
\widehat{G}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
induces a map from semisimple conjugacy classes in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\infty}
to those in
M_{N}(F_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})
. This map is injective except when
G=\mathrm{SO}^{\eta}_{2n}
, where its fibers are orbits under the outer automorphism. We will elide this technicality since everything in the
\mathrm{SO}_{2n}^{\eta}
case is up to outer automorphism and therefore consider an infinitesimal character of
G
as a semisimple matrix up to conjugacy: in other words an unordered sequence

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dotsc,\lambda_{N})

with
\lambda_{i}\in F_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
.

In the conjugate self-dual case, since the map
\widehat{G}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is a restriction from
{}^{L}\!{G}\hookrightarrow{}^{L}\!{G}_{N}
, it is determined by its first coordinate. Therefore, the infinitesimal character can also be represented by a semisimple conjugacy class in
M_{N}(F_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})
and therefore a similar unordered sequence.

It is also useful to package the tuple
\lambda_{v}=(\lambda_{1,v},...,\lambda_{n,v})
as the generating function
\sum_{j}X^{\lambda_{j,v}}
, which, by abuse of notation, we will also denote
\lambda_{v}
. In this way, if
\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i,v}[d_{i}]
is a local Arthur parameter such that each
\tau_{i,v}
has infinitesimal character
\lambda_{v}^{(i)}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}X^{\lambda_{j,v}^{(i)}}
, then we have the infinitesimal character assignment

(5.1.1)

\left(\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i,v}[d_{i}]\right)_{\infty}\mapsto\sum_{i}\lambda_{v}^{(i)}\sum_{l=1}^{d_{i}}X^{\frac{d_{i}+1}{2}-l}.

It can be seen from this that the character of
\tau[d]
determines that of
\tau
.

5.1.2.
Integrality

The infinitesimal characters of finite-dimensional representations of the
G_{v}
for
v|\infty
will play a special role. We express their classification in terms of generating functions.

For the self-dual case:

•

If
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
, then
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}
and the
\lambda_{v}
are elements of
1+\mathbb{Z}[X,X^{-1}]
invariant under
X\mapsto X^{-1}
and with each coefficient
0
or
1
.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}
, then
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
and the
\lambda_{v}
are elements of
X^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}[X,X^{-1}]
invariant under
X\mapsto X^{-1}
and with each coefficient
0
or
1
.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n}
, then
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{SO}_{2n}
and the
\lambda_{v}
are elements
\mathbb{Z}[X,X^{-1}]
invariant under
X\mapsto X^{-1}
and with each coefficient
0
or
1
except the constant coefficient which is
0
or
2
.

For the conjugate self-dual case:

•

If
N
is odd, then the
\lambda_{v}
are elements of
X^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}[X,X^{-1}]
with each coefficient
0
or
1
.

•

If
N
is even, then the
\lambda_{v}
are elements of
\mathbb{Z}[X,X^{-1}]
with each coefficient
0
or
1
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Call
\lambda_{v}

integral
if it is one of the cases above.

Note that this is the same as the
C
-algebraic condition of
[
BG14
]
.

5.2.
Central Characters

We collect some useful lemmas on central characters:

5.2.1.
Central Characters and Shapes

Every parameter
\psi
has a central character
\eta_{\psi}
, of order
\leq 2
in the self-dual case. Through class field theory, we will abuse notation and use
\eta_{\psi}
to denote the corresponding character of
\Gamma_{F}
. Note that

(5.2.1)

\eta_{\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}[d_{i}]}=\prod_{i}\eta_{\tau_{i}}^{d_{i}}.

5.2.2.
Central Characters and Transfer

We need the central character results described in
[
GGP11
, §10]
. We use throughout as in
[
KS99
, §5.1]
that any
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
comes with an injection
(Z_{G_{N}})_{\theta}\hookrightarrow Z_{G}
from the algebraic group of coinvariants.

First, as is known to experts:

Theorem 5.2.1
.

In the self-dual case, let
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
be either
\mathrm{Sp}^{\eta}_{2n}
or
\mathrm{SO}_{2n}^{\eta}
and
\varphi_{v}
be an
L
-parameter for
G_{v}
. Then the central character
\omega_{v}
of
\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\varphi_{v}}
is determined by
\omega_{v}(-1)=\epsilon(1/2,\varphi_{v})/\epsilon(1/2,\det\varphi_{v})
.

Proof.

This is claimed as an expected property in
[
GGP11
, §10]
. A proof follows from the main result of
[
LR05
]
together with a consistency statement between Langlands-Shahidi epsilon factors and those from Arthur’s classification (see e.g.
[
AHKO23
, B.2]
for a discussion).
∎

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Let
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
and
\varphi_{v}
an
L
-parameter for
G_{v}
. Then the central character
\omega_{v}
of
\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\varphi_{v}}
is constant on
\Pi_{\varphi_{v}}
(i.e. determined by
\varphi_{v}
).

Proof.

As explained in
[
Mok15
, Rmk 3.2.3]
, the endoscopic character identities
[
Art13
, Thm 2.2.1(a)]
and
[
Mok15
, Thm 3.2.1(a)]
together with the transformation property for transfer factors given by the generalization of 5.1.C in
[
KS99
, p.53]
can be used to show this for the central character restricted to the image of the injection
(Z_{G_{N,v}})_{\theta}\hookrightarrow Z_{G_{v}}
. In the conjugate self-dual case, this is all of
Z_{G_{v}}
.

In the self-dual case,
G_{v}
is abelian if
N=1,2
. When
N>2
,
Z_{G_{v}}
is trivial or
\pm 1
. It is trivial when
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
. Otherwise,
\omega(-1)=\epsilon(1/2,\varphi_{v})/\epsilon(1/2,\det\varphi_{v})
by Theorem
5.2.1
.
∎

We emphasize another consequence of the transfer factor transformation property after
[
KS99
, 5.1.C]
:

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Let
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
and
\varphi_{v}
an
L
-parameter for
G_{v}
. Let
\omega_{v}
be the common central character of
\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\varphi_{v}}
as in Lemma
5.2.2
.

Then,
\omega_{v}
restricted to the image of
(Z_{G_{N,v}})_{\theta}\hookrightarrow Z_{G_{v}}
uniquely determines the (conjugate) self-dual central character
\omega^{\prime}_{v}
of the representation
\tilde{\pi}_{\varphi_{v}}
of
\widetilde{G}_{N,v}
corresponding to
\varphi_{v}
.

With notation as in the statement of Lemma
5.2.3
, we will call this
\omega^{\prime}
the
transfer
of
\omega
. Note that because of the possible non-triviality of
\lambda_{C}
in the generalization after
[
KS99
, 5.1.C]
, the transfer
\omega^{\prime}
is not necessarily pullback to
Z_{G,v}
. In fact, in the self-dual case, the image of
(Z_{G_{N,v}})_{\theta}\hookrightarrow Z_{G_{v}}
is trivial, so
\omega^{\prime}_{v}
is actually completely determined by this
\lambda_{C}
and therefore
G
:

•

If
N
is odd,
\omega^{\prime}_{v}=\eta
for
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{N-1}^{\eta}
.

•

If
N
is even,
\omega^{\prime}_{v}=\eta
for
G=\mathrm{SO}_{N}^{\eta}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{v}=1
for
G=\mathrm{SO}_{N+1}
.

In the conjugate self-dual case, it can be checked by working over the algebraic closure that
(Z_{G_{N}})_{\theta}\hookrightarrow Z_{G}
is given on points
1
1

1

we ignore the difference between this map and its inverse which depends on the specific choice of transfer factors. In our eventual application, the distinction will be moot due to arbitrary choices of infinite-place isomorphisms
\overline{E}_{v}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
needed to parameterize infinitesimal characters.
by
\beta\mapsto\beta/\bar{\beta}
. By Hilbert 90, this is a bijection over local fields, so the map
\omega_{v}\mapsto\omega^{\prime}_{v}
is an injection.

•

If
N
is odd, the determinant of a conjugate symplectic/orthogonal parameter is the same type. Therefore, the image of
\omega_{v}\mapsto\omega^{\prime}_{v}
is all characters coming from conjugate symplectic or orthogonalparameters respectively depending on the types of parameters of
G
.

•

If
N
is even, the determinant of a conjugate self-dual parameter is always conjugate-orthogonal. Therefore, the image of
\omega_{v}\mapsto\omega^{\prime}_{v}
is all characters coming from conjugate orthogonal parameters and doesn’t depend on
G
.

In the case of
U^{+}_{N}
we can be even more precise:

Lemma 5.2.4
.

Consider
G=U_{N}^{+}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
. Then the transfer
\omega_{v}\mapsto\omega^{\prime}_{v}
is pullback through
Z_{G_{N,v}}\twoheadrightarrow(Z_{G_{N,v}})_{\theta}\xrightarrow{\sim}Z_{G_{v}}
.

Proof.

This follows by the endoscopic transfer identity
[
Mok15
, Thm 3.2.1(a)]
and
[
Mok15
, Thm 2.5.1(c)]
: if
\pi_{v}
has parameter
\varphi_{v}
, then
\omega_{v}
has parameter
\det\varphi_{v}
which necessarily lands in
{}^{L}\!{Z}_{G_{v}}
.
∎

5.3.
Shapes for the Self-Dual Case

We need a version of the theory of refined shapes for the self-dual case. Informally, a shape is a coarsening of the notion of an Arthur parameter, and corresponds to the part of discrete spectrum which we will isolate in our arguments. We use notation analogous to
[
DGG22
, §2]
and refer readers to
[
Art13
]
for more background. The key difference with
[
DGG22
]
is that the auxiliary data
\eta
will now be a central character instead of a parity.

5.3.1.
Cuspidal Shapes

One of the central results of the endoscopic classification,
[
Art13
, Theorem 1.4.1]
, is that each self-dual cuspidal parameter
\tau\in\Psi(N)
factors through a unique simple endoscopic datum
H
. We first show that for the parameters with which we are concerned, this group
H
is determined by the central and infinitesimal characters of
\tau
.

Proposition 5.3.1
.

Let
\tau\in\Psi(N)
be self-dual, cuspidal parameter with integral infinitesimal character
\lambda
at infinity and central character
\eta
. Then the endoscopic datum
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
through which
\tau
factors is entirely determined by
\lambda
and
\eta
.

Furthermore:

•

\lambda
is the infinitesimal character of a finite-dimensional representation of
H
.

•

If
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}
, then
\eta
is trivial.

•

If
G
is a form of
\mathrm{SO}_{2n}
, then
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n}^{\eta}
. In this case, for each
v|\infty
,
\eta
is trivial on
\Gal_{F_{v}}
if and only if
n
is even. In particular,
G
necessarily has discrete series.

Proof.

This is the argument of
[
CR15
, §3.11]
, but we allow for non-trivial central character.

Using
\lambda

We will see that
\lambda
determines
\widehat{G}
. First,
\lambda
is integral so by the version of Clozel’s purity lemma stated in
[
CR15
, Lemma 3.13]
, for each
v|\infty
, the
L
-parameter
\varphi_{v}:W_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathrm{GL}_{n}\mathbb{C}
associated to
\tau_{v}
is a direct sum of copies of the trivial representation
\mathbf{1}
, the character
\epsilon_{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}}:W_{\mathbb{R}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\times}\to\pm 1
, and, for
w\in\mathbb{Z}
, the representations

I_{w}=\Ind_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}^{W_{\mathbb{R}}}z^{-w/2}\bar{z}^{w/2}.

Note that
I_{w}=I_{-w}
and that
I_{0}=\mathbf{1}\oplus\epsilon_{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}}
.

Then
\lambda_{v}
is a list of numbers: a
0
for each
\mathbf{1}
or
\epsilon_{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}}
and a pair
(w/2,-w/2)
for each
I_{w}
. In addition
I_{w}
can be conjugated into an orthogonal/symplectic group if and only if
w
is even/odd. Since
\tau
comes from a simple endoscopic group that has either orthogonal or symplectic dual, it follows that all the
w
have the same parity.

In total, the non-zero numbers in
\lambda_{v}
are either be all integers or all half-integers for each
\lambda_{v}
, depending on whether
\tau_{v}
has image in an orthogonal or symplectic group, thereby determining
\widehat{G}
. In addition, it follows from our definition of integral that
\lambda_{v}
is the infinitesimal character of a finite-dimensional representation on
G
.

Using
\eta

Next,
\eta
determines the remaining part of the datum corresponding to
G
. By the discussion on
[
Art13
, p. 10]
, we have three cases:

•

If
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
, then
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}
and
\eta
is necessarily trivial.

•

If
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}
, then
G=\mathrm{Sp}^{\eta}_{2n}\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}
.

•

If
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{SO}_{2n}
, then
G=\mathrm{SO}^{\eta}_{2n}
.

This completely determines
G
. We can also compute
\eta|_{\Gal_{F_{v}}}
by computing
\det\tau_{v}
. In the
\widehat{G}\cong\mathrm{SO}_{2n}
case,
\tau_{v}
is a sum of
n
different summands
I_{w_{i}}
for
w_{i}
even and therefore acts trivially on
\Gal_{F_{v}}
if and only if
n
is even. Therefore,
G
necessarily has discrete series under our Assumption
2.3.3
.
∎

5.3.2.
Refined Shapes in the Self-Dual Case

Motivated by the above, we define:

Definition 5.3.2
.

An
refined shape
is a sequence

\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}

up to permutation and where
(T_{i},d_{i})
are positive integers,
\lambda_{i}
is an infinitesimal character of rank
T_{i}
, and
\eta_{i}
is a central character.

We say that
\psi\in\Delta
, or that
\psi
has refined global shape
\Delta
if
\psi=\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
with each
\tau_{i}
of rank
T_{i}
and such that each
\tau_{i}
has central character
\eta_{i}
and infinitesimal character
\lambda_{i}
at infinity.

Let
\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\Delta}
be the set of all elliptic, self-dual parameters on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
of refined shape
\Delta
.

Beware that
\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\Delta}
is empty unless all the bulleted conditions on
\lambda
and
\eta
of Proposition
5.3.1
hold.

Definition 5.3.3
.

The refined shape
\Delta
is
integral
if the total infinitesimal character of
\Delta
as in (
5.1.1
) is integral, in the sense of Definition
5.1.1
.

We also pick out a special refined shape:

Definition 5.3.4
.

Let
\lambda
be an integral infinitesimal character for a group of rank
N
and
\eta
a central character that is trivial if
\lambda
has half-integral entries. Then
\Sigma_{\eta,\lambda}
is the trivial refined shape
(N,1,\lambda,\eta)
.

5.3.3.
Properties

Proposition 5.3.5
.

Let
\Delta
be an integral refined shape of rank
N
such that all the
\lambda_{i}
are integral (in the sense of
5.1.1
) and
\eta_{i}
is trivial whenever
\lambda_{i}
has half-integer entries. Then there is a group
G(\Delta)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
such that all
\psi\in\Delta
are parameters for
G
. Furthermore,
G(\Delta)
has discrete series at infinity.

Proof.

Consider
\psi=\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}[d_{i}]\in\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\Delta}
. By the discussion on
[
Art13
, pp. 33-34]
, it suffices to show that
\Delta
determines two things:

•

whether each
\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
has image in an orthogonal or symplectic group. Let the total ranks of the orthogonaland symplecticpieces be
N_{O}
and
N_{S}
respectively,

•

the central character
\eta
of the sum of the orthogonal
\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
.

Then we will have

G(\Delta)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{SO}_{N_{S}+1}\times\mathrm{Sp}^{\eta}_{N_{O}-1}&N_{O}\text{ odd}\\
\mathrm{SO}_{N_{S}+1}\times\mathrm{SO}^{\eta}_{N_{O}}&N_{O}\text{ even}\end{cases}.

To show these two facts, apply Proposition
5.3.1
and note that if
d_{i}
is odd, whether
\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
has orthogonal or symplectic image is the opposite of that for
\tau_{i}
. If
d_{i}
is even, then it is the same. In addition, the central character of the sum of the orthogonal-image
\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
is determined through (
5.2.1
) by the central characters of the
\tau_{i}
and which
\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
have orthogonal image.
∎

This gives us two corollaries:

Corollary 5.3.6
.

Let
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
. Then

\Psi_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)=\bigsqcup_{\Delta:H(\Delta)=G}\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\Delta}.

Proof.

This follows from the above and the same discussion in
[
Art13
, pp. 33-34]
.
∎

Corollary 5.3.7
.

Let
\lambda
be an integral infinitesimal character of rank
N
. Then

G(\Sigma_{\eta,\lambda})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Sp}^{\eta}_{2n}&N\text{ odd}\\
\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}&N\text{ even, }\lambda\text{ has half-integer entries}\\
\mathrm{SO}^{\eta}_{2n}&N\text{ even, }\lambda\text{ has integer entries.}\end{cases}

In particular, for every
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
and infinitesimal character
\lambda
of a finite-dimensional representation of
G_{\infty}
, there is
\eta
such that
H(\Sigma_{\eta,\lambda})=G
(beware that
\Psi^{\Sigma_{\eta,\lambda}}_{\mathrm{ell}}
is empty when the conditions from Proposition
5.3.1
don’t hold).

We collect some more properties of refined shapes
\Delta
:

•

By
[
Art13
, (1.4.9)]
, All
\psi\in\Delta
correspond to the same pair
(\mathcal{S}_{\psi},s_{\psi})
up to conjugation. Call the common values
S_{\Delta}
and
s_{\Delta}
.

•

All
\psi\in\Delta
have the same Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
.

•

The infinitesimal character at infinity and central character of
\psi\in\Delta
is determined by formulas (
5.1.1
) and (
5.2.1
).

Finally, we relate integral refined shapes
\Delta
to a class of Archimedean parameters defined in
[
AJ87
]
, which we call AJ-parameters; see also
[
DGG22
, §4.3]
:

Lemma 5.3.8
.

Let
\Delta
be an integral refined shape. Then there exists an AJ-parameter
\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}
such that for all
\psi\in\Delta
,
\psi_{\infty}=\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}
. Furthermore, the induced localization map
\mathcal{S}_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{\infty}^{\Delta}}
is also determined by
\Delta
.

Proof.

This is more-or-less the same proof as
[
DGG22
, Lem. 4.3.4]

We have that
\psi_{\infty}
is determined by the
\tau_{i,\infty}
. However, checking the cases from
[
CR15
, 3.13]
,
\tau_{i,\infty}
is a discrete parameter with infinitesimal character matching that of a finite-dimensional representation so it is the unique discrete series parameter of its infinitesimal character
\lambda_{i}
. This forces
\psi_{\infty}
to be an AJ-parameter.

The localization statement comes similarly as in
[
DGG22
, Lem. 4.3.4]
by inspecting the formula
[
Art13
, (1.4.9)]
which holds both locally and globally.
∎

5.4.
Shapes in the Conjugate Self-Dual Case

For easy reference, we recall the definition of refined shapes in the conjugate self-dual case from
[
DGG22
, §4]
:

Definition 5.4.1
.

In the conjugate self-dual case, a
refined shape
is a sequence

\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}

up to permutation and where the
T_{i}
and
d_{i}
are positive integers,
\lambda_{i}
is an infinitesimal character of rank
T_{i}
, and
\eta_{i}=\pm 1
.

As the in the self-dual case, the
\eta_{i}
(which are now just a simple signs) are needed to determine
G(\Delta)
.

Definition 5.4.2
.

In the conjugate self-dual case, the refined shape
\Delta
is
integral
if the total infinitesimal character of
\Delta
as in equation (
5.1.1
) is integral.

As explained in
[
DGG22
, §4]
, all the analogous properties to §
5.3.3
for integral refined shapes hold in the conjugate self-dual case. The arguments are in fact simpler since all
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
have discrete series at infinity.

5.5.
Unrefined Shapes

Given a refined shape
\Delta
, we can forget some data to give an unrefined shape
\Box:=\Box(\Delta)=((T_{i},d_{i},\eta_{i}))_{i}
:

•

In the self-dual case, we forget all information about the infinitesimal character except whether it takes integral or half-integral values. For indexing consistency, we fold this information into
\eta
which now becomes a pair
(\omega,\pm 1)
of a quadratic central character and a sign.

•

In the conjugate self-dual case, we forget the infinitesimal character.

This still uniquely determines the group
G(\Delta)=G(\Box)
as in
[
DGG22
, Prop 4.3.1]
and Proposition
5.3.5
. We also define trivial unrefined shape

\Sigma_{\eta}:=\Box(\Sigma_{\lambda,\eta}).

Given infinitesimal character
\lambda
for
G(\Delta)_{\infty}
, Let
\Box_{\lambda}

be the set of
\Delta
with total infinitesimal character
\lambda
such that
\Box(\Delta)=\Box
. We will sometimes shorthand

(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{i})\in\Box_{\lambda}\implies(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}\in\Box_{\lambda}.

since
\Box
already contains the data of the
T_{i},d_{i},\eta_{i}
.

We define
\lambda[d]
to be the total infinitesimal character of the simple shape
(T,d,\lambda,\eta)
as in (
5.1.1
). Similarly, let
\boxtimes_{i}\lambda_{i}
be the total infinitesimal character of
(T_{i},1,\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}
—i.e. concatenating all the coordinates of each
\lambda_{i}
and ordering them increasingly.

Finally, to an unrefined shape
\Box=(T_{i},d_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}
we associate a “reduced group”

G_{F}(\Box):=\prod_{i}G((T_{i},d_{i},\eta_{i})).

This is not in general an element of
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
and can be thought of as the smallest group through which
\psi\in\Box
functorially factor. It will appear in bounds on growth rates of counts of automorphic reps
\pi
associated to
\psi\in\Box
.

5.6.
Norms of Infinitesimal Characters

To prove our bounds, we will compare two different norms of infinitesimal characters
\lambda
of some
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
:

•

the dimension of the finite-dimensional representation of
G
corresponding to
\lambda
:

\dim\lambda:=\dim^{G}\lambda:=C_{G}\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)}\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle

where the constant
C_{G}
only depends on
G
,

•

a minimum over positive coroots of
G
:

m(\lambda):=m^{G}(\lambda):=\min_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)}\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle.

Given an unrefined shape
\Box
, we can also define

\dim_{\Box}\lambda:=\dim^{G}_{\Box}\lambda:=\max_{(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\Box_{\lambda}}\prod_{i}\dim\lambda_{i}

Note that this can be
0
if
\Box_{\lambda}
is empty.

Given a group
G
, we can compute the number of positive roots:

P_{G}:=\frac{1}{2}(\dim G-\rank G).

This gives us a bound:

Lemma 5.6.1
.

\dim_{\Box}\lambda\leq(\dim\lambda)m(\lambda)^{P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-P_{G}}.

Proof.

The factors
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle
in the Weyl dimension formula for
\dim_{\Box}\lambda
are always a subset of those in
\dim\lambda
. However,
\dim_{\Box}\lambda
has
P_{G}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)}
fewer factors.
∎

6.
Weight Asymptotics for the Cuspidal Spectrum

6.1.
Setup and Trace Formula Decompositions

We now apply the techniques of
[
DGG22
, §5]
to compute our asymptotics. Fix either the self-dual or conjugate self-dual case. Let
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
. Choose:

•

a regular, integral infinitesimal character
\lambda
for
G_{\infty}
and
\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}
the associated Euler-Poincaré function, endoscopically normalized as in
[
DGG22
, §3.3]
,

•

a set of unramified places
S
and a test function
f_{S}
in the truncated Hecke algebra
\mathscr{H}^{\mathrm{ur}}(G_{S})^{\leq\kappa}
(see
[
ST16
]
),

•

a test function
f^{S,\infty}
at other places.

Choose
\Box=(T_{i},d_{i},\eta_{i})
a shape for
G
. We use the strategy of
[
DGG22
, §9]
to understand the asymptotics in
\lambda
of

I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{S,\infty}f_{S}):=\sum_{\Delta\in\Box_{\lambda}}I^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{S,\infty}f_{S}):=\sum_{\Delta\in\Box_{\lambda}}\sum_{\psi\in\Delta}I^{G}_{\psi}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{S,\infty}f_{S})\\
=\sum_{\Delta\in\Box_{\lambda}}\sum_{\psi\in\Delta}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{G}_{\psi}}m_{\pi}\tr_{\pi}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{S,\infty}f_{S}),

where
I^{G}_{\psi}
is the trace against the part of the discrete spectrum of
G
associated to
\psi
as in
[
DGG22
, §2.6]
through
[
Art13
, Thm 1.5.2]
or
[
Mok15
, Thm 2.5.2]
.

In actuality, we will instead use the stable versions:

S^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{S,\infty}f_{S}):=\sum_{\Delta\in\Box_{\lambda}}S^{G}_{\Delta}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{S,\infty}f_{S})\\
:=\sum_{\Delta\in\Box_{\lambda}}\sum_{\psi\in\Delta}m_{\psi}\epsilon_{\psi}(s_{\psi})|\mathcal{S}_{\psi}|\tr_{\Pi^{G}_{\psi}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f^{S,\infty}f_{S}),

which are expressed in terms of traces against
A
-packets and certain signs and constants that appear in the stable multiplicity formulas
[
Art13
, 4.1.2]
,
[
Mok15
, 5.6.1]
(note that we ignore the
\sigma
terms since all our parameters are elliptic).

The key formula for our final application is, the following, where we recall from §
5.5
that in the self-dual case
\Sigma_{\eta}
is the unrefined shape determined by a rank, a character
\eta
, and a choice of parity of entries of
\lambda
:

Proposition 6.1.1
.

In either the self-dual or conjugate self-dual case, let
\eta
be such that
G(\Sigma_{\eta})=G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
. Then for any infinitesimal character
\lambda
for
G
,

\sum_{\psi\in\Sigma_{\eta,\lambda}}\tr_{\widetilde{\pi}^{\infty}_{\psi}}(f^{\infty})=\begin{cases}(1/2)S^{G}_{\Sigma_{\eta}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}(f^{\infty})^{G})&G=\mathrm{SO}_{2n}^{\eta}\\
S^{G}_{\Sigma_{\eta}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}(f^{\infty})^{G})&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Proof.

This is an easier version of the argument in
[
DGG22
, Prop 5.3.1]
. We note that for simple shapes, the constants in the stable multiplicity formula are all trivial except for
m_{\psi}
which is
2
in the even orthogonal case.
∎

Following the above proposition, we wish to compute asymptotics in
\lambda
of
S^{G}_{\Sigma_{\eta}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}(f^{\infty})^{G})
. In the rest of this section, we show inductively that the main term in these asymptotics is a geometric term consisting of evaluation at central elements. This mirrors our previous strategy to compute level asymptotics in
[
DGG22
, §5]
. In Sections
7

and
9
, we will compute the relevant evaluations at central elements for the functions
\widetilde{E}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\infty}
and
\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\infty}
previously constructed.

6.2.
Geometric Estimation of Traces of
\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}

As input, we use the weight aspect bounds of
[
ST16
]
generalized to deal with nontrivial center as in
[
Dal22
]
.

6.2.1.
Computation of Constants

For
G/F
a reductive group,
\lambda
a regular integral infinitesimal character of
G_{\infty}
,
\omega_{\lambda}
the central character of the corresponding finite-dimensional representation of
G_{\infty}
, and
f^{\infty}
a test function on
G(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})
, the main term in our asymptotics is given by a linear combination of evaluation of
f^{\infty}
at central elements:

(6.2.1)

\Lambda(G,\omega_{\lambda},f^{\infty}):=\tau^{\prime}(G)\sum_{\gamma\in Z_{G}(F)}\omega^{-1}_{\lambda}(\gamma)f^{\infty}(\gamma).

Here,
\tau^{\prime}(G)
is a modified Tamagawa number from
[
ST16
, §6.6]
under canonical times Euler-Poincaré measure.

Note that in our cases,
Z_{G}(F)
always intersects the support of
f^{\infty}
in a finite set.

6.2.2.
Statement

Theorem 6.2.1

(Slight variant/Special case of
[
Dal22
, Thm. 9.1.1]
)
.

With notation as above, there are constants
A,B,C
with
C\geq 1
and depending only on
G
such that,

(\dim\lambda)^{-1}S^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})=\Lambda(G,\omega_{\lambda},f^{\infty})+O(m(\lambda)^{-C}q_{S}^{A+B\kappa}f_{S}(1)),

Where
\Lambda
is as in (
6.2.1
).

Proof.

Hyperendoscopy techniques as in the proof of
[
Dal22
, Thm. 9.1.1]
let us write
S^{G}
in terms of
I^{H}
for smaller groups. Then this follows from the exact same argument.
∎

6.3.
Preliminary Bounds for the Induction

Since we do not need to keep track of functions at split places, the bounds of
[
DGG22
, §7]
simplify. Recall the definitions of
\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{T}_{\Delta},\mathcal{T}_{i}:=\mathcal{T}_{\Delta,i}
from
[
DGG22
, §6.2.4]
.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

Let
\Delta=(T,d,\lambda,\eta)
be a simple shape and
f_{S}f^{S,\infty}
a trace-positive test function on
G(\Delta)^{\infty}
with
f_{S}
unramified. Then there is a test function
(f^{S,\infty})^{\prime}
on
G(1,d,\lambda,\eta)^{S,\infty}
such that

\left|S^{Td}_{\Delta}\left((f_{S}f^{S,\infty})^{Td}\right)\right|\leq S^{T}_{\Sigma_{\eta,\lambda}}\left((\mathcal{T}f_{S}(f^{S,\infty})^{\prime})^{T}\right).

Proof.

This is the same argument as
[
DGG22
, Prop. 7.3.2]
with empty
{S_{s}}
. We use a slightly modified version of
[
DGG22
, Lem. 6.3.7]
, noting that the proof does not actually require the exponent
d
.
∎

Proposition 6.3.2
.

Let
\Delta=(T_{i},d_{i},\lambda_{i},\eta_{i})_{i}
be a shape of rank
N
and
f_{S}f^{S,\infty}
a trace-positive test function on
G(\Delta)^{\infty}
with
f_{S}
unramified. Then there are test functions
f^{S,\infty}_{i}
on each
G(\Sigma_{\eta_{i},\lambda_{i}})^{S,\infty}
such that

|S^{N}_{\Delta}((f_{S}f^{S,\infty})^{N})|\leq\prodf_{i}S^{T_{i}}_{\Sigma_{\eta_{i},\lambda_{i}}}\left((f^{S,\infty}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S})^{T_{i}}\right)

Proof.

This is like the proof of
[
DGG22
, Prop 7.3.3]
: apply
[
DGG22
, Cor 7.2.2]
with empty
S_{s}
and then Proposition
6.3.1
.
∎

6.4.
Inductive Result

Now we can induct to get asymptotics of the contribution of
\Sigma_{\eta}
to the stable trace:

Theorem 6.4.1
.

Let
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
in either the self-dual or conjugate self-dual case and let
\lambda
be an infinitesimal character for
G
. Then there are
A,B,C
with
C\geq 1
such that

(\dim\lambda)^{-1}S_{\Sigma_{\eta}}^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})\\
=\begin{cases}\Lambda(G,\omega_{\lambda},f^{\infty})+O(m(\lambda)^{-C}q_{S}^{A+B\kappa}f_{S}(1))&G\neq\mathrm{SO}_{2,\mathrm{triv}}\\
0&G=\mathrm{SO}_{2,\mathrm{triv}},\end{cases}

where
\Sigma_{\eta}
is the unrefined trivial shape such that
G(\Sigma_{\eta})=G
and
\Lambda(\cdot)
is given by (
6.2.1
).

In addition, if
\Box
is an unrefined shape and
G=G(\Box)
,

(6.4.1)

(\dim_{\Box}\lambda)^{-1}|S_{\Box}^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})|\leq C(G,\Box,f^{\infty})+O(m(\lambda)^{-C}q_{S}^{A+B\kappa}f_{S}(1))

for some inexplicit constant
C(G,\Box,f^{\infty})
depending only on the arguments.

Proof.

This is the same induction-on-classification-rank argument as in
[
DGG22
, Thm. 9.3.2]
. First,
[
DGG22
, Lem. 9.3.1]
reduces to the case where
f^{\infty}
is trace-positive. In the self-dual case, there are three bases cases (those when
G
is abelian):

•

N=1
. Then
\Sigma_{\eta}
is trivially the only shape so the bound follows from a very special case of Theorem
6.2.1
.

•

N=2
,
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2}^{\mathrm{triv}}
. Then there are no simple parameters since
{}^{L}\!{G}
embeds into a split torus.

•

N=2
,
G=\mathrm{SO}_{2}^{\eta}
,
\eta\neq\mathrm{triv}
: then the only parameters with non-trivial shape are those for
\eta_{1}\boxtimes\eta_{2}
with
\eta=\eta_{1}\eta_{2}
. Since each component
(\eta_{i})_{v}
for
v|\infty
is valued in
\pm 1
, these only appear in our traces for finitely many
\lambda
. Therefore, we can bound
S^{G}=S^{G}_{\Sigma_{\eta}}+E
for an error
E
that is constant in
\lambda
and apply Theorem
6.2.1
.

In the conjugate self-dual case, the bases cases are
N=1
and
G=U^{\pm}_{1}
which follows as in the
N=1
case above.

For the inductive step, first note that by
[
DGG22
, 5.3.1]
or an analogous statement in self-dual setting:

S_{\Sigma_{\eta}}^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})=S^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})-\sum_{\Box\neq\Sigma_{\lambda}}S_{\Box}^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty}).

If we could show that the terms in the rightmost sum the have smaller growth in
m(\lambda)
than
\dim(\lambda)
, then Theorem
6.2.1
would prove the bound we want for
\Sigma_{\eta}
. For this, outside of our base cases, we always have
P_{G_{F}(\Box)}<P_{G}
so
\dim(\lambda)^{-1}\dim_{\Box}(\lambda)=O(m(\lambda)^{-1})
by Lemma
5.6.1
. In particular, knowing the second bound would imply that all the terms in the sum are lower order in
m(\lambda)
and still exponential in
q_{v}^{\kappa}
by
[
DGG22
, Lem. 6.2.3]
.

Therefore, it suffices to show the second bound (
6.4.1
). For this,

|S_{\Box}^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})|\leq\sum_{\Box_{\Delta}=\Box}|S^{N}_{\Delta}((f_{S}f^{S,\infty})^{N})|

Each summand can be bounded by Proposition
6.3.2
, again using
[
DGG22
, Lem.6.2.3]
to bound
\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S}(1)
:

\prodf_{i}S^{T_{i}}_{\Sigma_{\eta_{i},\lambda_{i}}}\left((f^{S,\infty}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S})^{T_{i}}\right)\\
=\left(\prod_{i}\dim\lambda_{i}\right)\left(\prodf_{i}\Lambda(G_{i},\omega_{\lambda_{i}},f^{S,\infty}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S})+O(m(\lambda)^{-C}q_{S}^{A+B\kappa}f_{S}(1))\right)\\
\leq\dim_{\Box}(\lambda)\left(\prodf_{i}\Lambda(G_{i},\omega_{\lambda_{i}},f^{S,\infty}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S})+O(m(\lambda)^{-C}q_{S}^{A+B\kappa}f_{S}(1))\right)

by the inductive hypothesis.

The result then follows by noting that
|\Box_{\lambda}|
is bounded above by a constant only depending on
\Box
and taking a maximum over all constants that are in the exponent of
q_{v}
. Note that we can remove the dependence of the
\Lambda(G_{i},\omega_{\lambda_{i}},f^{S,\infty}_{i}\mathcal{T}_{i}f_{S})
on
\omega_{\lambda_{i}}
through upper-bounding the sum defining it by a sum of absolute values.
∎

6.5.
From
S
to
I

For future use, we note that the bound also applies to
I
-terms, even though we will not use it in this specific paper.

Theorem 6.5.1
.

Choose
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(N)
and let
G=G^{*}
in the self-dual case or an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}
in the conjugate self-dual case. If
\lambda\in\Box_{\lambda}
, the bounds in Theorem
6.4.1
hold with the
S^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})
replaced by
I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})
after also replacing all
f^{\infty}
terms on the right with
(f^{\infty})^{G^{*}}
.

Proof.

First, note that the case of non-quasisplit
G
is conditional on
[
KMSW14
]
and its publicly unavailable reference “KMSb”.

For the proof, we apply
[
DGG22
, Cor 10.2.2]
(or its exactly analogous self-dual version), referring to the notation therein and noting that

I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})=\sum_{\Delta\in\Box_{\lambda}}\sum_{\pi_{0}\in\psi^{\Delta}_{\infty}}\tr_{\pi_{0}}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda})m^{G}(\pi_{0},\Delta,f_{S}f^{S,\infty}).

If
\Box
is stable (in particular when it is
\Sigma_{\lambda}
), the expansion in
[
DGG22
, Cor 10.2.2]
only has one term with
s=1
so
I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}f_{S}f^{S,\infty})=S^{G^{*}}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}(f_{S}f^{S,\infty})^{G^{*}})
.

Otherwise, this follows from applying Theorem
6.4.1
to each term of
[
DGG22
, Cor 10.2.2]
or its self-dual analog. The dominant contribution comes from
s=1
.
∎

Note
.

For a shape
\Box
with some
d_{i}>1
, there is a subset
\Phi_{\Box}\subseteq\Phi_{+}(G)
such that
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle
is constant over
\alpha\in\Phi_{\Box}
. The
\alpha\in\Phi_{\Box}
are exactly those not appearing in the computation of
\dim_{\Box}\lambda
.

An inspection of the argument above shows that the error term
O(m(\lambda)^{-1})
in Theorems
6.4.1
and
6.5.1
can be improved to an analogous
O(m_{\Box}(\lambda)^{-1})
where
m_{\Box}(\lambda)
only minimizes over
\alpha\notin\Phi_{\Box}
. This is irrelevant to the current paper where we only consider
\Box=\Sigma
.

7.
Shalika Germs and
(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}

To apply Theorem
6.4.1
, we need to understand the main term (
6.2.1
) given by a sum over central elements for
(\tilde{f}^{\infty})^{G}=(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}
, the function whose trace is a count of automorphic forms weighted by their root number. In this case, we wish to show that the main term (
6.2.1
) vanishes.

7.1.
Transfers at Non-Regular Elements through Shalika Germs

In this section, we consider the general problem of understanding values of transfers at central elements. Consider a (possibly twisted) reductive group
\widetilde{G}
and
H\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(\widetilde{G})
. We will eventually apply this to
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{G}_{N}
and
H
a classical group as in §
2.2.2
.

We first note that for central
\gamma\in H
,

(\widetilde{f}^{\infty})^{H}(\gamma)=\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}((\widetilde{f}^{\infty})^{H})=\mathcal{SO}_{\gamma}((\widetilde{f}^{\infty})^{H}).

If
\gamma
were strongly regular, then we could understand this through the standard orbital integral transfer identities as in
[
KS99
]
:

(7.1.1)

\mathcal{SO}^{H}_{\gamma}((\widetilde{f}^{\infty})^{H})=\sum_{i}\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}(\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma}_{i})\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{G}}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}(\widetilde{f}^{\infty}),

where
\gamma
is a norm of the stable class containing the rational-class representatives
\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}
and the
\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}
are a choice of transfer factors. These would allow to evaluate
\mathcal{SO}_{\gamma}((\widetilde{f}^{\infty})^{H})
in terms of orbital integrals of
f^{\infty}
. For arbitrary semisimple elements, doing so is much more complicated.

The standard technique (e.g.
[
Kot88
, §3]
) to resolve this issue is comparing Shalika germ expansions. This was implemented in full generality
[
LS07
, Thm 2.4.A]
for non-twisted endoscopy. We mimic the argument in the twisted case using a comparison of transfer factors which was the main technical result of
[
Wal08
]
. This argument is known to experts and seems implicit in the stabilization of the twisted trace formula—we just reconstruct it to deduce some bounds from the details.

We first recall the definition of non-regular stable orbital integrals:

Definition 7.1.1
.

Let
H/F_{v}
be quasiplit reductive and
\gamma\in H
an arbitrary semisimple element. If the
\gamma_{i}
are representatives for the rational classes in the stable class of
\gamma
, then

\mathcal{SO}_{\gamma}:=\sum_{i}e(H_{\gamma_{i}})\mathcal{O}_{\gamma_{i}},

where
e(H_{\gamma_{i}})
is the Kottwitz sign of the necessarily connected
H_{\gamma_{i}}
(i.e.
(-1)^{r}
where
r
is the difference between the split ranks of
H_{\gamma_{i}}
and its quasisplit inner form).

The main goal of what follows is Proposition
7.1.6
. For this, we compare two Shalika germ expansions of (
7.1.1
): with respect to
H
on the left-hand side, and with respect to
\widetilde{G}
on the right-hand side. The latter relies on a descent property for twisted transfer factors, which we begin by recalling below.

7.1.1.
Descent for Twisted Transfer Factors

The descent property we need is the main result of
[
Wal08
]
; an English-language summary of a special case can be found in
[
Var09
]
. We begin with a very general setup:
G/F_{v}
is an arbitrary reductive group, and we let
\widetilde{G}:=G\rtimes\theta
for an automorphism
\theta
fixing a pinning. Let
H\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(\widetilde{G})
be such that there is a transfer map defined to
H
instead of to a
z
-extension (this will be satisfied for all
(\widetilde{G},H)
appearing in our self-dual and conjugate self-dual cases).

Fix semisimple
\tilde{\gamma}\in\widetilde{G}_{v}
and
\gamma\in H_{v}
such that
\gamma
is the norm of
\tilde{\gamma}
(i.e.
\tilde{\gamma}
is the stable class that is the transfer of
\gamma
). As explained in
[
LS07
, §1.3-4]
, since
H
is quasisplit, we can without loss of generality replace
\gamma
by a stable conjugate so that
H_{\gamma}=H^{0}_{\gamma}
is quasisplit. Then
[
Wal08
, §3.5-6]
constructs an
\bar{H}
such that:

•

\bar{H}
is an endoscopic group of
(G_{\tilde{\gamma}}^{0})^{\mathrm{sc}}
(Waldspurger more precisely constructs the entire endoscopic quadruple).

•

(H^{0}_{\gamma})^{\mathrm{sc}}
is a quasisplit “non-standard endoscopic group” for
\bar{H}
as in
[
Wal08
, §1.7]
. In particular, there is transfer of stable conjugacy classes from
\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}
to
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}
constructed from an isomorphism of tori contained in rational Borels (note that this factors through the canonical projection
\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}\to\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma,\mathrm{sc}}
).

Now, choose strongly
\widetilde{G}
-regular
X_{H}\in\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}
. Then
X_{H}
transfers to a stable class
X_{\bar{H}}
in
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}
and further to a stable class in
\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
. Choose
X_{G}
in this stable class of
\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
. We can decompose it as

X_{G}=\widetilde{X}_{G,\mathrm{sc}}+\widetilde{X}_{G,z},\qquad\widetilde{X}_{G,\mathrm{sc}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma},\mathrm{sc}},\;\widetilde{X}_{G,z}\in\Lie(Z_{G_{\widetilde{\gamma}}}).

Finally, we let
\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}
and
\Delta^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma},\mathrm{sc}}}_{\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}}
be the transfer factors from
[
KS99
]
for the groups/Lie algebras determined by the sub/super-scripts (but normalized as in
[
Wal08
]
without the
\Delta_{IV}
-factor).

Theorem 7.1.2

(
[
Wal08
, Thm 3.9]
)
.

With notation from above, there is a choice of transfer factors and a neighborhood
0\in U\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}
such that for
X_{H}\in U
,

\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}(\exp(X_{H})\gamma,\exp(X_{G})\widetilde{\gamma})=\Delta^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma},\mathrm{sc}}}_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}}(X_{\bar{H}},X_{G,\mathrm{sc}}).

Proof.

The statement of
[
Wal08
, Thm 3.9]
is an equality of relative transfer factors which, as a note afterwards points out, can become an equality of transfer factors after making the correct choices. We also comment on notation for the reader’s convenience: our
\widetilde{\gamma}
is Waldspurger’s
y\eta y^{-1}
and our
\gamma
is their
\epsilon
. Our assumptions on
H
let us ignore all subscripted
1
’s.

We write out Waldspurger’s map
\varphi
explicitly as it is defined before Remark 3.8. Similarly, we explicitly write out
\bar{G}[y]
as it is defined at the end of §3.5.
∎

As a nice corollary:

Corollary 7.1.3
.

With notation from above, assume that
\dim H_{\gamma}=\dim G_{\widetilde{\gamma}}
. Then
\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}(\exp(X_{H})\gamma,\exp(X_{G})\widetilde{\gamma})
is constant for
X_{H}
close enough to
0
.

In particular, we can define
\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}(\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma})
to be this constant value.

Proof.

Such corollaries were originally pointed out in
[
LS07
, §2.4]
: by the dimension assumption,
\bar{H}
is necessarily an elliptic endoscopic group of
(G^{0}_{\widetilde{\gamma}})_{\mathrm{sc}}
of the same dimension, so it is necessarily the quasisplit inner form. Therefore
\Delta^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma},\mathrm{sc}}}_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}}(X_{\bar{H}},X_{G,\mathrm{sc}})=1
always. The result follows from Theorem
7.1.2
noting that different choices of transfer factors differ by a constant value.
∎

7.1.2.
Shalika Germs

We recall some standard material on Shalika germs (see e.g.
[
MW16
, §II.2]
). Let
\widetilde{G}=G\rtimes\theta
be a possibly twisted reductive group over
F_{v}
and fix semisimple
\tilde{\gamma}\in\widetilde{G}_{v}
. For any test function
f
on
\widetilde{G}_{v}
, there is a neighborhood
1\in U_{f}\subseteq G_{v,\tilde{\gamma}}
on which the Shalika germ expansion holds: if
\mathfrak{u}_{f}=\exp^{-1}(U_{f})\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
, then for all regular semisimple
X\in\mathfrak{u}_{f}
,

(7.1.2)

\mathcal{O}_{\exp(X)\tilde{\gamma}}(f)=\sum_{U\in\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}})}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}}_{U}(X)\mathcal{O}_{\exp(U)\widetilde{\gamma}}(f)

where
\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}})
is the set of nilpotent
G_{v,X}
-orbits in
\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}
and
\Gamma^{\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}}_{U}(X)
is the Shalika germ—a smooth function on
\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{rss}}
independent of
f
such that for all
\alpha\in F_{v}^{\times}
:

(7.1.3)

\Gamma^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}}_{U}(\alpha^{2}X)=|\alpha|_{v}^{-\dim\mathcal{O}_{U}}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}}_{U}(X).

7.1.3.
Comparing Germ Expansions

We can now mimic the argument of
[
LS07
, 2.4.A]
. Fix a reductive, possibly twisted,
\widetilde{G}=G\rtimes\theta
over
F_{v}
,
H_{v}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(\widetilde{G}_{v})
and semisimple
\gamma\in H_{v}
.

Let
T_{H}
be a maximal torus of
H_{v}
that is also an elliptic maximal torus of
H_{\gamma}
. Let
j_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq l
be representatives for the
H_{v}
-conjugacy classes of admissible embeddings
T_{H}\hookrightarrow H_{v}
.

For any test function
f
on
G_{v}
, there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq T_{H}
such that the Shalika germ expansion holds for
f^{H}
on each
j_{i}(U)
. Then for strongly regular
X_{H}\in\mathfrak{u}=\exp^{-1}(U)
,

(7.1.4)

\mathcal{SO}_{\exp(X_{H})\gamma}(f^{H})=\sum_{i}\sum_{U\in\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma_{i}})}\Gamma^{\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma_{i}}}_{U}(j_{i}(X_{H}))\mathcal{O}_{\exp(U)\gamma_{i}}(f^{H})

where we use the shorthand
\gamma_{i}:=j_{i}(\gamma)
.
This gives us:

Lemma 7.1.4
.

Choose a test function
f^{H}
on
H
, semisimple
\gamma\in H
, and strongly regular
X_{H}\in\mathfrak{t}_{H}
(i.e. the Lie algebra of our elliptic torus
T_{H}
of
H_{\gamma}
). Then the function

r(\alpha):=\mathcal{SO}_{\exp(\alpha^{2}X_{H})\gamma}(f^{H})

for small enough
\alpha
is of the form

r(\alpha)=C\mathcal{SO}_{\gamma}(f^{H})+\sum_{d_{i}}C_{d_{i}}|\alpha|_{v}^{-d_{i}}

for
C\neq 0
, some set of
d_{i}>0
, and arbitrary constants
C_{d_{i}}
.

Proof.

This is the intermediate result of
[
Kot88
, §3]
up until the third equation on page 639. Summarizing the argument, Kottwitz computes the homogeneous of degree-
0
part of (
7.1.4
) after applying (
7.1.3
): first, the main result of
[
Rog81
]
computes all the
\Gamma_{1}
terms on the elliptic torus
T_{H}
. Then, formulas from
[
Kot86
]
simplify the remainders into the definition of the non-regular stable orbital integral
2
2

2

We warn that this definition is not simply the sum of orbital integrals and instead involves “Kottwitz signs” of centralizers as coefficients.
.

Kottwitz uses a clever choice of measure normalization to simultaneously remove the formal degree of the Steinberg from the formula in
[
Rog81
]
and to simplify the relation between embeddings
j_{i}
and the non-regular stable orbital integral.
∎

Fix
X:=X_{H}\in\mathfrak{t}_{H}
such that
\exp(\alpha^{2}X)\gamma
is strongly regular for some open set of small enough
\alpha
. Then, setting
\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}=\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(\gamma)
, we get the other side of the comparison from (
7.1.1
):

(7.1.5)

\mathcal{SO}_{\exp(X)\gamma}(f^{H})\\
=\frac{D^{G}(\widetilde{\gamma}\exp(\tilde{\jmath}(X)))^{1/2}}{D^{H}(\gamma\exp(X))^{1/2}}\sum_{i}\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}(\exp(X)\gamma,\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(\exp(X))\widetilde{\gamma}_{i})\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(\exp(X))\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}(f),

where we again use Waldspurger’s convention for transfer factors (leaving out the
\Delta_{IV}
term) and the
D
terms are Weyl discriminants:

D^{H}(\gamma)=|\det(1-\Ad(\gamma)|_{\mathfrak{h}/\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}})|_{v}

(note that the
D^{G}
-term is stable-conjugacy invariant and therefore independent of the suppressed
i
).

We can further restrict our choice of
X
so that the quotient of
D
-terms simplifies to an expression

\frac{D^{G}(\widetilde{\gamma})^{1/2}}{D^{H}(\gamma)^{1/2}}\frac{D^{\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}}(\tilde{\jmath}(X))^{1/2}}{D^{\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}}(X)^{1/2}}

that involves a similar term for Lie algebras:

D^{\mathfrak{h}}(X)=|\det(\ad(X)|_{\mathfrak{h}/\mathfrak{h}_{X}})|_{v}.

By Theorem
7.1.2
, for small enough
X_{H}
, the transfer factors in (
7.1.5
) simplify to

\Delta^{\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{i},\mathrm{sc}}}_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}}(X_{\bar{H}_{i}},\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(X)_{\mathrm{sc}})

after constructing appropriate
\bar{H}_{i}
that is an endoscopic group of
(G_{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}^{0})_{\mathrm{sc}}
as in §
7.1.1
and where
X_{\bar{H}_{i}}
is the (non-standard) transfer of
X
to
\bar{H}_{i}
.

We can then Shalika-germ expand again, this time on
\widetilde{G}
and around
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
; for
X
small enough, the identity (
7.1.5
) becomes:

(7.1.6)

\mathcal{SO}_{\exp(X)\gamma}(f^{H})=\frac{D^{G}(\widetilde{\gamma})^{1/2}}{D^{H}(\gamma)^{1/2}}\frac{D^{\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}}}(\tilde{\jmath}(X))^{1/2}}{D^{\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}}(X)^{1/2}}\\
\times\sum_{i}\sum_{U\in\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i},\mathrm{sc}})}\Delta^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i},\mathrm{sc}}}_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}}(X_{\bar{H}_{i}},\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(X)_{\mathrm{sc}})\Gamma^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}}_{U}(\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(X))\mathcal{O}_{\exp(U)\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}(f).

We recall:

Lemma 7.1.5

(
[
Fer07
, Lem. 3.2.1]
)
.

Let
G/F_{v}
be a reductive group and
H\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. Then for any choice of Lie algebra transfer factors, there is a quadratic character
\chi_{G,H}:F_{v}^{\times}\to\pm 1
such that the transfer factors satisfy:

\Delta(\alpha X_{H},\alpha X_{G})=\chi_{G,H}(\alpha)\Delta(X_{H},X_{G})

for all
\alpha\in F_{v}^{\times}
.

In particular, since the quadratic character
\chi_{G,H}
vanishes on
\alpha^{2}
, the summand in (
7.1.6
) for a single pair
(i,U)
scales with
\alpha
like:

(7.1.7)

\frac{D^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}}(\alpha^{2}\tilde{\jmath}(X))^{1/2}}{D^{\mathfrak{h}_{\gamma}}(\alpha^{2}X)^{1/2}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i},\mathrm{sc}}}_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}}(\alpha^{2}X_{\bar{H}_{i}},\alpha^{2}\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(X)_{\mathrm{sc}})\Gamma^{\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}}_{U}(\alpha^{2}\tilde{\jmath}_{i}(X))\\
=C_{i,U}|\alpha|^{-\dim\mathcal{O}_{U}+\dim G_{\tilde{\gamma}}-\dim H_{\gamma}}

for some constant
C_{i,U}
and small enough
\alpha
.

7.1.4.
Results

As a consequence that is surely known to experts but does not seem to be written down anywhere, we get the following key input for the vanishing of the main term in our global trace formula expressions:

Proposition 7.1.6
.

Let
f
be a test function on
\widetilde{G}_{v}
and
\gamma,\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}
as above. Assume that for all
i
and all nilpotent orbits
U
of
\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}
,

\dim\mathcal{O}_{U}=\dim G_{\widetilde{\gamma}}-\dim H_{\gamma}\implies\mathcal{O}_{\exp(U)\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}(f)=0.

Then
\mathcal{SO}_{\gamma}(f^{H})=0
.

(Note that if
\dim G_{\widetilde{\gamma}}=\dim H_{\gamma}
, we only need to check that
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}(f)=0
.)

Proof.

This comes from comparing the expansion in Lemma
7.1.4
to equations (
7.1.6
) and (
7.1.7
) for
X:=X_{H}\in\mathfrak{t}_{H}
. Note that we get much stronger information about the terms in (
7.1.7
), in particular, that many of them need to vanish. However, we don’t actually need this extra information.
∎

We can also derive a more refined bound which we expect to be useful in a future work studying the level-aspect version of this problem:

Proposition 7.1.7
.

Let
f
be a test function on
\widetilde{G}_{v}
and
\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
as above. Assume that for all
i
and all nilpotent orbits
U
of
\mathfrak{g}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}
such that

\dim\mathcal{O}_{U}=\dim G_{\widetilde{\gamma}}-\dim H_{\gamma},

there is a bound

|D^{G}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{i})^{1/2}\mathcal{O}_{\exp(U)\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}(f)|\leq M.

Then we can bound

|D^{H}(\gamma)^{1/2}\mathcal{SO}_{\exp(X)\gamma}(f^{H})|\leq MC_{G_{v},H_{v}}

for some constant
C_{\tilde{G}_{v},H_{v}}
depending only on
v,\tilde{G},H
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be the set of such
(i,U)
. Comparing Lemma
7.1.4
and (
7.1.6
),

|D^{H}(\gamma)^{1/2}\mathcal{SO}_{\exp(X)\gamma}(f^{H})|\leq M\left|\sum_{(i,U)\in\mathcal{U}}C_{i,U}(f)\right|,

where the
C_{i,U}
are as in (
7.1.7
). The number of terms in the sum can be bounded by a constant depending only on
G
so it suffices to bound each term. Furthermore, since local fields have finitely many extensions of each degree, there are only finitely many possibilities for the pair
(H_{\gamma},G_{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}})
so it suffices to allow the
C_{i,U}
bound to also depend on these centralizers.

For our special
(i,U)\in\mathcal{U}
where (
7.1.7
) is locally constant, we compute
C_{i,U}
by plugging in a small enough fixed
\alpha_{0}^{2}X_{0}
. “Small enough” depends on the neighborhood
U
in Theorem
7.1.2
and the validity of the Shalika germ expansions. All these only depends on
H_{\gamma},G_{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}},H,G
. In other words, there is a small enough
\alpha_{0}^{2}X_{0}
depending only on
H_{\gamma},G_{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}},H,G
. This finishes the argument.
∎

Finally, we get an exact formula in special cases—a twisted version of
[
LS07
, Lemma 2.4.A]
:

Corollary 7.1.8
.

Let
f
be a test function on
\widetilde{G}_{v}
and
\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
as above. Assume that
\dim G_{\widetilde{\gamma}}=\dim H_{\gamma}
. Then

\mathcal{SO}_{\gamma}(f^{H})=\sum_{i}e(G_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}})\Delta^{\widetilde{G}}_{H}(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}_{i})\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}(f),

where
e(\cdot)
is the Kottwitz sign (and recalling the definition of transfer factors at these potentially non-regular elements from Corollary
7.1.3
).

Proof.

We again compare Lemma
7.1.4
to (
7.1.6
). The argument of
[
LS07
, 2.4.A]
exactly carries over after replacing their discussion involving 1.6.A with Corollary
7.1.3
. We point out that the signs
e(G_{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}})
again come from
[
Rog81
]
through another use of the aforementioned argument in
[
Kot88
, §3]
and that the right-hand side should be though of as the “correct” definition of a non-regular
\kappa
-orbital integral.
∎

7.2.
Application to
(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}
: Self-Dual Case

We apply the result of
7.1.1
to
show the vanishing at central elements of the function
(\widetilde{E}^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{G}
defined in
4.3
.

In this section, we will only study the self-dual case with
N
even and
G=\mathrm{SO}_{N+1}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{sim}}(N)
where root numbers are most interesting. The conjugate self-dual case is significantly more computationally involved and will be put off until next section.

Note first that the class
1\rtimes\theta\in\widetilde{G}_{N}
is the one that transfers to
1\in G
since
\theta
preserves the standard pinning. To eventually use Proposition
7.1.6
, we note:

(G_{N})_{1\rtimes\theta}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Sp}_{N}&N\text{ even}\\
\mathrm{O}_{N}&N\text{ odd}\end{cases}.

Furthermore, when
N
is even, the stable class of
1\rtimes\theta
is the same as its rational class since
